THE RATES.

The over prodcce of the Borough Rate last year, which is available this year, accounts for £1,230, and the same cause in the Sewers Rate for £312. The total of these three items provides for £4,186 of the deficit and after a further slight adjustment has been made by the Overseers in connection with their balance in hand, we shall require only £2,200, which can be met by levying an Improvement Rate of 3s 21 (an increaEe of 2d), and a General Sewers Rate of 4d (a reduction of 2d) and a Poor Rate of 4s fid (an increase of 2d). This makes the total rates for the ensuing sear

Bs. AS AGAINST 7s. 10d. and I think you will agree with n:e that although increases are at all times to le very much deplored, yet considering the great increase in our requirements, it is a matter for congratulation that we have been able to provide for our necessities with an increase of only 2d. in the £. (Applause.) A further matter for con- gratulation is thit we have been able to reduce our calls upon the trading undertakings, as the Gas Depart- ment have Leen called upon for £2,000 less, the Tramways Departm.nt for £.500 less, and the Electricity Department for £5OO less, this latter department for this year contributing nothing. There is one

WORD OF WARNING

that I should like to utter before closing, and that is to ask you to note that the loans taken up on the rate accounts during the year total to £90,000 and the repay- ments during the year only amount to £13,400. Some of this is due to the clearing up of expenditure incurred several years ago, but there are so many schemes on hand at the present moment that ib behoves all of 113 to proceed very carefully in anything requiring the expenditure of capital moneys, as next year we shall not be in as favourable a position as we are to-night. It might be possible for us in the future to obtain some slight further assistanze from the Government, now our rate for -Elementary Education is on the border line of is. 6d. in the but there is no doubt that the provision of new schools will be a

SEVERE STRAIN ON THE RESOURCES OF THE BOROUGH for some years to come. (Hear, hear.) The financial year just ended has been a memorable one in many respects, bnt I think that on the whole the town has enjoyed a reasonable measure of prosperity, a clear index to which is the passenew receipts irJra the tramways which show an increase of £970. I trust that the ratepayers of the t )wn will be able to bear the addi- tional burden which has been placed upon them with a feeling of satisfaction, that the increase is only a small one as compared with our increased expenditure. Finally, I have to again expres3 my indebtedness to the efficient staff of officials, and especially to Mr.

Fairhurst and Mr. Woodcock. As members of com- mittees, we do what we can to control and supervise expenditure, but much of the responsibility and a great deal of economical working of the large concerns of this Corporatien must rest upon the officials, and this town is to be congratulated that we have in our Finance Department, two such capable and efficient chiefs as those I have mentioned. They have given me every assistance, and we are all indebted to them for their devotion to the interests of the whole town. (Applause). Alderman Bk:NNETT said it had come to the Council with a sense of relief that the actual rise of the rates was only twopence. Frankly he was afraid it would be something more, though not as large es some of their friends outside were disposed to say. He thought it was better that the committee should keep their own counsel, and that the pleasure of making such announce-, meats as Connncillor Wilkinson had been able to maker should be reserved for Budget night. Taking a broad survey of the situation, he did not think there was any cause for alarm. It might be that in the future the tendency would be upwards, particularly with regard to education and possibly with regard to other features of municipel management, because they were face to face with great and necessary improve- ments, and if they were to maintain their reputation as a propressive town they could not shirk them. At all hazards they must not, for the mere sake of economy, neglect to keep the town up to date. There was no reason why they should be depressed cr down-hearted, but there was every reason why they should to careful in spending their money .with true .economy The elasticity of their productive undertakings wa• a source of great encouragement. lie had alwayss been a great believer in municipal trading and had never been able to see why municipalities should not only be able to give excellent services but to provide substantial profits on ccncerns like gas, water, electricity and trams. He hoped that in a few years they would go a great deal further and that other sources which he would not ettempt to indicate would provide profits on municipal trading and help still further to relieve the heavy burden of the rates. Ile wondered what would have been the position if they had followed the advice of some of their friends and reduced the price of gas to such a point that they would have hen unable to contribute is in the £ta the rates. It was a sound policy under existing con- ditions to reserve a portion of their profits for the reduction of rates. Although their expenditure was hound to increase in view of all the undertakings in front of them, they might reasonably hope that the rateable value of the borough and the profits from municipal undertakings would also increase. His great hope was that some day a Govern- ment would arise that was wise enough to see that the existing apportionment of the burden of municipal and imperial Government was better adjusted. They had not yet found a man brave enough to tackle the question. It was, he believed, bound to be dealt with before long. He had always felt that the true basis was to be found in taxing according to a man's capacity to pay—in other words, something like universal income tax. It might be di ffi• cult to apply it to muni zipal purposes, but it would be infinitely more logical than the present methods. It sometimes happened that men in receipt of substantial incomes were rated at a very low sum and had not to pay anything like their proper proportion, whilst others, such as struggling shopkeepers, were often very heavily mted and found it difficult to make ends meet. The only way to obtain a readjustment was for the c.ouncils of the country to make a noise about it and let the authorities in London know that they were not going to be treated with indifference any longer. Alderman JAMES EVANS referred to Councillor Wilkinson's advice to the committees of the Corpora- tion to exercise every legitimate economy. He (the speaker) believed the committees had done remarkably well in the past year, in face of many difficulties about which the majorlty of the population knew nothing. The increase in the rates was not due to the joint action of com- mittees of the Corporation, but was caused by the education question alone. But for that expendi- ture, instead of twopence beirg put on the rates, they would have been taking twopence off. (Hear, hear.) That was evidence, that, under the Mayors guidance, the Corporation had striven to do its work economically, wisely and well.ln the course of , the discussion on the Education Budget, the Chairman of the Education Committee threw the whole of the responsibility for the financial rositicn of education locally, on the Town Council. The point be (Alderman James Evans) would emphasise was that in the coming year they would have the opportunity of dealing with such financial matters, and he hoped they would keep their eyes on those little items which were perhaps a little outside education proper,and could be regarded as luxurious additions. They would have to do with education what they had to do with other municipal matters—content themselves with what they could afford to pay for. To go faster than that, meant the placing of burdens on the backs of those who were not well able to hear them. With regard to the increase of .Cl3l on the Health Committee's estimates, be claimed

OUR RESOURCES."

Tuesday was Budget night at the meeting of the Warrington Town Council and the Chairman of the Finance Committee (Councillor John Wilkinson), submitted a statement showing that an increase of rates to the extent of 23. in the 1 is required to meet the demands of the various committees for the coming year. The amount is made up as follows : s. d. Improvement Rate (increase of 2d.) ... 3 2 Sewers Rate (reduction of 2d.) 4 Poor Rate (increase of 2d.) ... ... 4 6

Total ... •.. .., 8 0 In the course of the discussion stress was laid upon the ever-increasing cost of education and the familiar plea for larger grants from irnper:al sources was once more reiterated. It was stated that the provision of new schools would be a severe strain on the resources of the borough for some years to come. Councillor WILKINSON, in his address to the Council, said :—For the fourth time I have the honour to submit for your approval the estimated expenditure for the year. I suppose that you are all prepared to hear from me to-night that we must look for a rdatively large increase in our rates to meet our growing expenditure. The local prophets have already to retold this and no doubt you have come in a properly resigned frame of mind to meet the worst. Well, while Amitting that our rites must be increa9ecl and without for the moment disclosing to what extent, I will lighten the burden resting on your minds by stating at Once that the prophets are all wrong. I would have you notice that we are faced with a 0110 WING EXPENDITURE Itil ALMOST EVERY' DEPARTMENT and especially expenditure in a certain direction—l mean the salary or wage bill. The increasing cost of living almost compels an increase in wages, and the determination of the Town Council that every man whom it employs shall have a minimum living wage— s most righteous determination by the way—has to be -paid for,but I take it that the Council sill not hesitate to pay the piper seeing that in this respect it hss so resolutely called the tune. In presenting to the Council this evening the estimates provided by the various committees, I feel that my first dn'3 ought to be one of congratula- tion to the spending committees who have endeavoured with so much success to keep their expenditure within the limits prescribed by the estimates for 1911-12. (Hear, hear.) The committees under the head of the Borough Rate all show a surplus, and although the balance in hand has been reduced by .ClOO, this is more than accounted for by the clearing oil of the deficiency of £542 on the Parr Hall account, which this has been thought a favourable opportunity to get rid of. The satisfactory working referred to above, enables us to make a clear start with the estimates for the ensuire, year, and you will find that the amount required from the overseers is £41,403, as against .239,166 for 1911-12, an increase of £5,237. We were all very much disappointed with the estimates of the Education Committee—(hear, hear)—showing an increase of £3,300, hut some of th:s was to be expected because of the remission of e'ementary school fees and the fact that the Exchequer contributions remain practically on the came basis as they were ten years ago, whilst our expAnditure in corsequence of the provision of new schools has gone up by leaps and bounds. The argument that by

ABOLISHING reße we should so increase the attendance that the Govern- ment Grant would fully compecsate for the loss of fees, has not been jus,tified at all—(hear, hear)—though on this point it must be admitted that the medical inspection of the school children is accountable for some of the absentees. Yet all is not lost. If the town has not received the £l,BOO it has, of course, been retained by the parents, who have benefited. I need not dwell further upon the sum required for education purposes as the matter wes fully dealt with a few weeks ago by the chairman of the Education Finance Sub-committee, but I should like you to remember that although the amount required from the rates for educational purposes has increased from £10,408 in 1904 5 to £23,500 in 1912-13,iper3enta ge of increase of 125, the Government contributions have only increased from £27,142 to £29,671, an increase of 9 per cent only. (" Shame.") The remaining increase in expenditure of £1,737 is mainly on account of ' the increased requirements of the Watch Department, the increased loan charges amounting to £341 for the extension of the Baths, and £566 for the new bridges ; and a sum of £428 for salaries. A portion of the latter sum will, however, be recouped fro-a the Trading Departments. There are some small increases on various accounts in the Finance Department, but these are balanced by the saving in respect of the differential rating under the Warrington Extension Order 1896, which expired in November list. The call on the overseers (244,103) is equivalent to a rate of 3s 4d in the £ (exclusive of the Guardians and the Overseers requirements), and is required for the following purposes : Amoant Rate in required. the £. d.

It was not, however, the sort of finance that a public body ought to embark upon. The Council ought to realize that it was there to protect the public. The figures which Councillor Wikinson had submitted with regard to education and the proportions in which the cost was borne by the local authorities am! the State were appalling. They ought to " raise Cain " over a thing like that. The Council ought to be furnished with the Budget a week in advance, instead of on the day before the Council meeting. The MAYOR : It cannot be got out until the 31st March. Councillor PEMBERTON said he defied any accountant in England to ascertain from the Budget the position that the Corporation was in. He moved an amendment that the rates for the coming year be not more than is 10d in the £.

Ths MAYOR : You cannot move an amendm nt. You can vote against the proposition. Councillor HENSIIA LL said he agreed with Councillor Pemberton on some points. If it were practicable to maintain a rate of 7s 10d or, better still, to reduce it, the Council would, like Barkis, all be willing to. (Hear, hear.) He would, however, rather t die a Mark Tapley , attitude and think that there was in the Budget a good I deal of ground for satisfaction. Dealing with the con- tinual advance in the cost of education, Councillor Henshall felt like flies on a wheel. Not only was the increase terrible, but they were apparently unable to control it. They had taken £3,000 less from the profit-earning departments for the relief of rates, whilst there was £3,500 more to find for education. That was roughly equivalent to a 7d. rate, but there wts only 2d. of a rise, and at the same time all the legitimate and essential expenditure of the Corporation—including that on new bowling greens, widening of the streets, and erect:on of bridges—was i being met. The growth of the town justified them n expecting a constantly increasing rateable value. On the question of education the Council were relatively powerless. They could only deal with it by gripping the Government by the throat, as they were the offenders and sinners. De submitted that, reviewing the whole situation, things were not nearly so bad as they might be. Councillor IlawzitortN said that not only had the Council a grievance against the Imperial Exchequer on

the ground of education, but they were also entitled to some consideration with regard to the grants from Road Boards. He had never been able to understand why the County Councils should allocate to themselves the whole of those grants, while an important borough like Wargrington, with miles of main road and the responsibility of providing a new bridge to link up Lancashire and Cheshire, received nothing towards that huge expenditure. Further, the Imperial Exchequer was authorized this year to make grants far sanatoria throughout the country. Warrington had a sanatorium but not a red cent had been received from the Govern- ment towards it. He did not agree with Councillor Pemberton as to the wisdom of exploiting the reserve fund of the electricity and tramways undertaking for the relief of rates. It was important that that depart- ment, above all others, should have a decent reserve fund. They had recently found that it would be better to scrap a great deal of the plant at the works end instal new appliances. An expenditure of thousands of pounds on the tram track would probably be necessary, and how were they to meet such items without a fund to fall hack upon? The department was more or less in an experimental stsge and changes kept coming into vogue to lessen the cost of production. Councillor WILKINSON, in reply iag to the diecussior, said he wished Councillor Pemberton we aid come down to solid facts. With reference to his argument in favour of filching the reserves of trading concerns, the Finance Committee 51wa3s asked : "What can your undertakicg stand ? They had thoroughly considered what the concerns might have to do in a few years and satisfied themselves that ccntributicns to the rates could be taken without in any way weakening the concerns. Before long there would to big alterations at the electricity station and they would be in a far tetter position if they had a box full qf money t) ray for the work, instead of having to take it straight from the rates. Another thing that surprised him was how men were continually cryir g Rates ! Rates ! Rates ! " He would like to point out that before a landlord could legitimately raise the II rent of a is home by 2,1. per week there would have to be an increase in the rates ol 2s. A man with ten houses might begrudge paying Is 88, a week extra in his rates towards the educaticn of the children of his less fortunate brethren. If a Sbylock landlord wanted his pound of flash, let him put 2cl co the rent, and the tenant who had three children would save 9d a week in school fees. Who was being hurt ? Nobody. Rates could only stand a certain strain but if they brought them below a certain level, poverty stared the town in the face ; there was nothing going on at all. Take any town in England that was low-rated and they wruld find it was a poverty-stricken town. They could get a rate to a height at which it would break people's backs, but Warrington's rates had not reached that stage by along way. Apart from education, the muni- cipal rate was about fis 3d in the £, and that would bear good comparison with the average rates for the past EO years,despite the improvements which bad been effected and the advantages enjoyed to-day, " Warrington to-day," he concluded, amid same applause," is as sound, if not sounder, than ever it was, in its history." The formal resolutions fixirg the rates at ds in the £ were then approved.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

In place of the usual dramatic fare, patrons of the Bylands-street house have this week had the opportunity of seeing some of the latest triumphs in 1 animated photcgraploy. Mr. Will Onda's pictures are well yelled, highly realistic and full of interesting and exciting situations. " Retaliation" is the title of the chief film. It is a thrilling novel in picture and holds the audience spell-bound. A story of love and duty is well brought home in the picture "A. Physician's Honour." and of course there is the inevitable Wild West film in "The Bandit's Mask," a well thought out story and an excellent production. "A Mender of Nets" is sure to please even the most critical, for there is a good dramatic flavour about it and the place chosen for its enactment is quite in accordance with the plot. An exciting story of love and scandal is displayed in "The Little Organist,' and the audience follow each movement with rapt interest. Amongst the humorous films depicted are "A Losing Game," "Bill and the Lions," "Arabella loves Her Master," and " Wonderful Equilibrium." Miss Sara Silcock, the well-known Warrington soprano, renders two songs each evening in a very artistic and pleasing manner. NEXT WEEK.

For next week the management have secured the comedy dram a " I rom Mill r,to Mansion "the story of a great strike.

THE DIOUSME." (THE MAIDS OF JAPAN).

"The Mousme" (the Maids of Japan), Mr Robert Courtneidge's latest musical comedy success, will make their first bow to the Patrons of tte Royal Court Theatre, on Monday, April 15th. The joint authors of the play are Mr. Alex M. Thompson and Mr. Robert Courtneidge who have had the valuable collaboration of Mr. Arthur Wimperis and Mr. Percy Greenback with the lyrics, and Mr. Lionel Monckton and Mr. Howard Talbot with the music. It is the largest Musical Comedy Co. travelling at the present time, numbering 75 artistes. " I consider that The Mousme ' is by far tbs best play dealing with Japanese life that has yet been produced in England, "says Baron Ito, son of the late Prime Minister of Japan." The scenery, in particular, is typically Japanese, and the artist has succeeded in portraying the delicate tints of Japanese scenery in a manner I have never yet seen equalled on the English stage. The dresses, especially those of the ladies, are also excellent, their colouring reminding me vividly of the general scheme of dress colour in Japan." "In other Japanese plays I have seen in andon, I have frequently noted that those responsible for the dressing of the play, have erred on the side of making the colour scheme too gaudy.Novvta anyons who knows Japan, this is a serious mistake to make, for, as a ration, neither the men nor the women of Japan are prone to wear gaudy colour-4, their favourite tones be:ng far more subdued than is the case with other Eastern nations I feel bound to say that 'The Mousme ' provides the best picture of Japan I have yet seen on the English stage.'

BOWLING.

WORKS AMATEUR BOWLING ASSOCIATION.

Mr. Patrick (hon. seo.) has again sent out circulars inviting competitions for the annual Inter-works Cup Competition for teams of 12 aside. All entries must be in writing and sent in by 8 o'clock on Saturday even- ing, April 13th. Handicapping of teams and draw will take place on Monday,April 15th, at headquarters. First round to commence on Saturday, April 20th. All players to be registered at the same time of entering teams and any works having more than one team entered, the players for each team must be registered separately to the Secretary.

POSITIVE WARRINGTON WITNESS.

A Warrington witness, who gave her evidence years ago, now endorses it in a most positive way, adding immeasurably to its valve. Mrs. S. Howell, of 13, Albert-street, Bewsey-road, Warrington, says :—" I've been wonderfully better in health since I used Dean's backache kidney pills. I took them for the kidneys, which had been causing me a great deal of misery for about six months. The trouble started with lumbago. I was quite well cn the Sunday, and on the Monday I couldn't move. The pains in my back and kidneys were frightful I had to keep to bed, but was quite unable to turn over " The kidney secretions were disordered. There were also bad headaches. I suffered terribly with these at times, and also with nervousness, dizziness, and a constant tired feeling, _

" I kept to my bed for four or five weeks, and then got up, but was still bad. Then two months afterwards I wos laid up again for thrao %%eekrti The symptoms were just the same as before. " The doctor said my heart was very weak. It felt very tad, at times fluttering a great deal and then almost standing still.

"When I first began using Dean's backache kidney pills, I noticed that ease came after the first four or five doses ; and after thit I steadily improved, until I became quite well. "I would strongly advise the use of these pills to all kidney sufferers, for they are excellent. You can tell how vs ell I am now by my being rale to do my washing again, which before I had been compelled to give up. (Signed) SELINA HOWELL." Nearly four pears lat.r, Mrs. Howell said :—" Doan's pills cured me some time ago, and lam as well now as I have ever been in my life."

WOMAN SUNTRAGE : THE NEXT/STEP. The National Union of Women's Suffrage Societies declares that, now the Conciliation Bill has been defeated, its first object will be to strengthen the support of an amendment to the promised Government Reform Bill. The committee feel that the responsi- bility for ensuring the passage of such an amendment is laid upon those who opposed (and with the aid of the militants defeated( the Conciliation Bill because they preferred a wider measure.

[BY REDCAP].

From the " Feathered World."

These are one of the most useful breed of fowls we have. In appearance they should be similar to the Barred Rock, except, of course in colour cnly. They are a very handsome fowl, with their rich lemon feather colour and bright yellow legs and beak. The Buff Rocks are most adaptable for the cottager and farmer, their colour not being at all suitable for town runs. They are excellent winter layers of large tinted eggs. One of my readers (who keeps a small pen of this variety) tells me that he has not been without eggs all the past winter. He further states that he has kept no other breed for the past 12 years, and does not anticipate making a change. This is a very good recommendation for the Buff Rock, and one which the writer can bear testimony to ; having kept them with satisfactoty results. At the present time they are not very popular. But this is only because their utility qualities are not sufficiently known. For beauty and ,utility combined the Buff Rock is not behind many of the lother more popular breeds.

YOUNG STOCK MOST PROFITIM.E.

One reason for White in actual results from poultry farming is because a gocd many of those who keep poultry do so without systematically weeding out the old stock hens. The most profitable plied in the life of a laying hen is during the first year of her existence. And they should never be kept after this age, unless, of course, they are exhibition stock. For utility par , poses more profit is made during the period named. It is obvious that I mean that such profit is derived from the egg supply. There is still another potent reason why they should net be kept after the age rueotiened. After y. u have got the best from the hens in the shape of eggs, they, being yuung, fetch better prices for table purposes than do old hens. As a matter of fact, a twelve-month-old hen should "dress" fairly well for culinary purposes. And when fed rapidly on good food they are almost equal to a young cockerel. As soon as they go off laying and before they m ult they should be fatted up for market ; thus you will, by this method, make more in actual profit than by keeping them ( n in the hope that. they will soon commence laying again. Don't lose sight of the fact that even while they are idle the food bill goes on, and up goes your expenditure.

lIINTS TO T-lIE DACKYATIDEII. Now the weather is somewhat milder, you may sow mustard, rape and cress seed in shallow boxes. This will soon come up, (especially if placed in a sheltered corner) and will be much enjoyed by the brood of chicks. I take it that nearly all backyard pcultry farmers like to rear a brood or two of chicks. It is a very interesting pursuit, and if you care for their wants in the manner described, you will be more than repaid in assisting them to early maturity. See that all the chicken coops are kept scrupously clean, and that the floor of the coop is made vermin proof. If you are at all troubled with rats, you should prevent them from taking the chicks'. Once they get hold of one, they will never rest until they take the lot. If you are using bottomless coops, it is a good plan to nail a picce of fine-mesh wire netting across the bottom of the coop ; this does not prevent the chicks from having the benefit of the earth floor, but at the same time it is a good preventative against a visitation from rats. Of all pests, rats are the most destructive to the poultry farmer. I know that your space is limited, but move the chicken coop about on to fresh ground as often as it is possible. Tainted ground is very detrimental to young chicks. When the coop is moved you should beat the ground it has been resting on, by forking it up and spi inkling a few handsful of quick lime into the soil. Let your chickens get as much of the sun as possible, for it helps them in grow th wonderfully. They should he kept free from cold winds, aLd not allowed to occupy wet runs ; chickens soon contract colds and cramp, and it is very disappointing when one loses the chicks so long waited for, and from which much is expected.

Dill CHICK I c OD. Nearly every poultry keeper is by now convinced of the benefit derived in rearing young stock principally on dry chick food. Personally Ido not recommend to free a use of soft food for chickens, and even when this diet is given it should not be too sloppy. Providing you obtain the best brands, I know of no better food for chicks than the above. Where chickens have a free run and where they are able to get plenty cf green food and insect food, they nred little else other than dry chick food. A well blended sample of this food is a very natural diet for the chicks, and when one observes the natural tendencies of feathered life,theargument in favour of of using dry chick food is very convincing, for it contains just those elements which would be obtained by natural pursuits. There is less danger of diarrhcca when feeding on this diet than there is if soft food is too frequently used, besides all this one could not wish for a more handy diet. It is also most inexpensive, inasmuch as one eightpenny bag of " Artnitage's Dry Chick Food" will feed twelve chicks until they are three weeks old. I have used this food and can thoroughly recommend it to my readers. In conclusion, Dry Chick Food has been proved a great boon to the poultry farmer.

- - rEATIIER EATIYG One of my readers complains of his hens pecking one another. Be ask for the cause and a remedy ? This very distasteful practice is caused through shortage of green food and animal food. Give your fowls plenty of green food. And give them a feed of bullock's liver about every other day, or get a few meaty bones from your butcher for them to peck at. Follow out these instructions and they will soon step leather picking. If however, as sometimes happens, some of the fowls are more addicted to this practice than the others, isolate the most criminal ones, place them in separate coops, and feed as eifected. And after a period of isolation try them again amongst the rest of the stock. If they return to the had habit, remove them again instantly, and so continue until cured.

WHAT BREED ? Another reader is desirous to know tv hat breed to take up for a town run. He statcs that the fowls would have access to a large run, but the run is situated right in the town. The mcst suitable breeds for a town run are Croad Langshans, Black Minorcas, Black Leghorns or Black Wyandottes. Any of the foregoing beeeds are good layers, and because of their dark plumage they are most suitable for town runs.

FUNERAL OF INSPECTOR R. ROPER, ST. HELENS.

--- The funeral of Inspectcr Robert Roper, late of the Ist Kir g's Dragoon Guards, who was a member of the St. Helens Borough Force for over twenty-six years, took place at St. Helens Cemetery on Saturday. The remains were escorted to the burying place by a detach- ment of the borough police under Chief Constable Ellerington and Chief Inspector Goodall. Inspectors Holding and Fairhurst and Sergeants Small, Hurst, Heaton, and Gleave were the bearers, and there were also present Detective-inspector Dunn, Sergeants Staveley, Curran, and Roe. The service was conducted by the Rev HE Hopkins, curate of the Parish Church.

ST. HELENS SWAMP BARROW.

The return League fixture between the above teams attracted a fair crowd to the Knowsley-road ground, but the fare provided was of a one-sided character. As a result of several bouts of passing Greenwood and Prescott rated over the Barrow line with a try each, Turtill converting one. Near the interval Dobson placed a goal for Barrow from a free kick. With the wind at their backs upon resuming St. Helens had matters all their own way, and Turtill quickly dropped a goal. A procession of tries followed for the Saints. Shallcross running over with three, Greenwood two, and Prescott and Waddell one each, whilst Turtill distinguished himself by kicking five goals and Molyneux one. Result —St. Helens 8 goals 9 tries (33 points), Barrow 1 goal (2 points). HULL KINGSTON ROVERS v WIDNES.

A big crowd witnessed this match at Craven-street, keen interest being shown in the struggle which the Rovers are making for position in the League. They bad no difficulty with Widnes, although victory was not achieved unt I a bright and fast game had been played. In the first half tries were scored for the Rovers by Brain, Huskins and Mann, two of which were improved by Carmichael. On the other side Widnes had a couple of penalty goals, kicked by Jones, the half-time score being 13 points to I. After the interval tries by McDonald and Sandham were improved by Carmichael, who also added a penalty goal; and just on time Aspey scored a try for Widnes, Jones failing at goal. Result: —Hull Kingston Rovers 5 goals, 5 tries (25 points), Widnes 2 goals 1 try (7 points).

Warrington Fully Extend Oldham. The Warrington team was somewhat of a scratch side on Monday evening, when Oldham were entertained in the postnoned Lf ague mach. Shugars, Cheaters, Fearnley, Jolley, and Dickenson were all absentees, and a number of "A" team men had to be introduced. Oldham bad their full cup team out, with the exception of Wood and GNV Smith. The kick-off was at 530, and the evening beautifully line, but, only about 2,000 spectators paid for admission. The teams were as follows:— Warrington: Tilley, full back ; Waywell, Mclntyre, Renwick, Woodburn, three-quarter backs ; Daintith, Jordan, half backs; G Thomas, Bradshaw, J Tranter, Arnold, Skelhorn, P Boardman, forwards. Oldham: G Cook, full back; T Young, S Deane, E Davies, T J NVilliams, three-quarter backs; E Anlezark, J Lomas, half backs; J Ferguson, A E Avery, IA Smith, F NV Wise, T McCabe, J Wright, forwards. Referee: Mr Tonge, Swinton. Fergusson started and Woodburn immediately ran to the Oldham quarter where Davies kicked badly into touch. Avery broke sway finely in the loose, but was splendidly tackled by Tilley in the home half. Cook was next prominent with a smart run from full-back, and a pass to Young. The latter kicked across and caught the Warrington defence napping, but Loma was offside under the posts. Arnold was applauded for a fine piece of forward play which resulted in Davies being captured close in, but Cook cleared again in brilliant style. Oldham now began to show superior form, and weak tackling by the locals gave the visitors a footing in the home quarter. Here Lomas burst through and passed forward to Dean, who raced over near the posts, the Warrington men strongly protesting against the legality of the point. Mr Tonge allowed the try, however, and Lomas kicked an easy goal. The home team shaped indifferently for a spell after this, and Davies broke through with a second try after being twice tackled. Lomas missed the goal. The crowd now infused some spirit into Warrington, and Mclntyre made a great solo effort which took him to the line, and only a bad bounce of the ball prevented a score under the posts. ,Oldham were now held, and a penalty for obstruction was next convertod by Renwick. Warrington attacked again with great energy, and the ball was passed across to Woodburn, who was upset at the corner flag. Renwick dropped at goal from an awkward angle, and the ball passed just outside. The visitors now invaded, and Tilley making a poor clearing kick, Warrington were forced to the line. Anlezark dived over from the next scrum. but the goal was missed. Thomas led the way back and charged Young's k'ck near the line. The ball went over and Thomas claimed for the try, but it was a near thing and the referee was against him. Half-time : Oldham, 11 points ; Warrington, 2 points The second half opened with some exchange punting, and gradually Warrington assumed the upper hand. Mclntyre again made a great effort, but had no luck, and Avery returned strongly to the Warrington line where Anlezark was only just kept out. Fine work by the Warrington forwards carried play to the Oldham quarter again, where Daintith passed to Mclntyre, who dashed through the Oldham backs and scored a great try under the posts, for Renwick to goal. Play now became very exciting, and though Arnold was off injured for some time, the Warrington men kept Oldham on the defensive. Poor half back play caused chances to be wasted, but Waywell had a great opportunity on the left, which a resolute player would have turned to good account. As it was Cook saved under the posts. Renwick had another shot at goal, which missed narrowly, and then the forwards rushed the ball to the line in thrilling style, Cook saving luckily from Skelhorn and Thomas. Oldham cleared by powerful punting, but could not cross the centre, and Jordan raced back again almost to the line before being downed by Lomas. Just on time Renwick punted beautifully into the goal mouth, and A Smith made a line catch in the midst of the Warrington forwards. A miss, and Oldham had lost. Final ; Oldham, 1 goal, 3 tries (II oints) ; Warrington, 2 goals, 1 try (7 points).

LANCASHIRE SHIELD FINAL.

BALROW "A" BEAT RUNCORN "A." The Lancashire Shield changed hands on Saturday, when Barrow "A " inflicted a decisive defeat on Runcorn "A" at Warrington, in the final round. Both teams were strongly represented, and contained several senior team players. There were 2,000 spectators. Runcorn started with the wind and at once set up a strong attack, Bate and the veteran Walker serving the ball well from the base of the pack. Smart passing eventually enabled Jones to run over, but Massey failed at goal. Hannah made a fine effort on the left tor the Barrow men, but long punting by Runcorn drove them back. Hoxworth was nearly over for Ruacorn, and another attack was checked by Morgan intercepting and running well into the Runcorn half. The interval came with Runcorn leading by 3 points to nil. In the second half the Barrow men soon showed that they meant business, and aided by the wind they forced Runcorn back to the line. Fine forward play kept Runcorn penned to their own quarter, and eventually a cross-kick saw Hannah field smartly and race over with an equalising try. Runcorn were now badly over- played, though Walker strove hard to got his backs going. A mistake by Bate let in Narey, who picked up smartly and a sharp sprint ended in a try near the posts and Narey placed the Furness men further ahead. The Cestrians made futile efforts to pierce the Barrow defence and at the close the Northeners had gained a popular victory. Scores: Barrow, 2 goals 2 tries (ten points) ; Runcorn, one try (three points). PRESENTATION OF THE SHIELD. A large crowd assembled in front of the grandstand at the close, when the shield was presented to the captain of the winning team by Mr. Tonge, of Swinton, viee-president of Lancashire County,who congratulated both teams on the sportsmanlike way they had played.

MANCHESTER LEAGUE. RYLANDS RECS V. WEAST. Weaste were the visitors to the Rylands Rees enclosure on Saturday. The locals were the first on the attack and Griffiths opened the scoring the goal kick failing. This was quickly followed by another score. this time by Hennessey. The visitors pressed, and following Galley making dead, Brown notched a goal from a frre kick. Shortly before the interval Morris scored through amis-kick on the part of the visitors' full-back. Half-time Rylands, 9 points; Weaste, 2 points.—ln the second half Rylands had matters practi- cally their own way and were continually on the attack. Early on llennessey scored ; Ltah made an excellent run and passing to flerfessey that player went over for a try. Just before time was called Hennessey got over at the corner, the goal:kick being a failure. Result : Rylands, 6 tries, 1S points; Weaste, 2 points.

, LIVING PICTURES AND JUVENILE • CRIMINALITY. Mr Stuart Deacon, the Liverpool stipendiary magis- trate, speaking on Monday at the annual r.eeting of the Liverpool Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children, spoke of the increase in the number of young people brought up at the police court on charges of petty larceny, and slid that with astonishing frequency the explanation offered by the offenders was that they stole the money in order to visit the picture shows. In other cases he had had experience that the character of the pictures exhibited had proved an incentive to juvenile criminality. Tie did not condemn picture exhibitions, as he thought they were capable of provid- ing admirable entertainment ; but parents should be warned on the subject, and ought to be wary about the character of the shows they permitted the children to see. Mr J 0 Legge, the director of education for Liverpool, spoke of the evil influence of the disgraceful Illustrated publications which were sold to children.

The Rev. W. H. Claxton, nho since October Ist, 1903, has been pastor of the New Jerusalem Church, Kearsley (and who has lectured at Warrington on behalf of the New Church), has resigned his pastorate in consequence of hislmceptance of the position of missionary minister for the London district of the New Church. at the invitation of the Missionary and Church Extension Board of that church.

THE INFIRMARY.

ADVANCE IN INCOME BUT MORE STILL REQUIKED.

THE EFFECT OF THE INSURANCE ACT.

" Although the institution is in ifs 103rd year, it ahows every sign of vigour," said Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., C.V.0., in his presidential address at the annual meeting of governors of the Warrington Infirmary and I Dispensary held at the Town Hall on Thursday after- noon of last week. ,The remark was fully borne out by the record of sustained activity presented in the annual report. Sir GILBERT GREENALL, Bart., C.V.0., presided, and I there were also present :—Mrs Willis, Mrs Pigot, Mr W Peter ltylands (chairman of the Board of Manage- ment, Mr George Dakin (hon treasurer), the Mayor Councillor Dr Joseph),Revs Canon Willis,W Bracecamp, H V Pigot, J Yonge, W Reid,H Ogden,Very Rev Father P Wh ttle, Aldermen T Burton (chairman of the Workingmen's Committee), W J Forshaw, J Smethurst and A Bennett, Councillors H Roberts and J Pemberton Messrs 3 II Smethurst, C White, John Fairclough, Vere B Davies, H Collin, Henry Greenall, A J Jolley, W N Hutchiugs, J Fulton Smith, C Ford, L Weir,H Hope, A C Binks, J Moore Murray, II Rogers, and J H J Hampson (secretary). PRESIDENT ON THE WORK OF THE YEAR. The PRESIDENT, in the course of a short speech, described the Infirmary as the greatest institution in the borough. The total number of patients treated during the year was 11,931, of whom 938 were in-patients. The operations —many of them of a serious character—numbered 514. Of the 938 ic-patients, there were only 32 deaths—a very low average which reflected the greatest credit cn the house surgeons and staff to whom grateful thanks were due for the devotion and skill which they gave to the large number of patients who came under their charge. (Applause.) The ordinary expenditure for the year was £49l6—a very moderate sum for the amount of work which they did because, in addition to treating and maintain- taming the patients, they had to keep a very large staff of employees of various kinds to do the necessary work of the institution. They were pleased to note that the ordinary income showed an increase of £145 on the previous year. It was very gratifying to observe that right through the time of the extension, and since, the income from reliable sources had shown a uniform and substantial increase. They felt particularly grateful to the Workingmen's Coe mittee for their continued hand- some support of the funds. During the year tbey bad contributed £1,221 and at their last annual meeting the contribution for the current year amounted to £1,397 93 10d —a very gratifying increase. (Applause.) 'I hey were looking forward in the future to increased help from that source. The self-sacr:ficing work of the Million Penny Fund Committee deserved their very best thanks. They were looking forward hopefully to a speedy and successful termination of their labours, AT LEAST £5,000 A YEAR WAS REQUIRED to carry on the work of the institution, and as the ordinary income last year was £4,410, the Board earnestly appealed to the public to provide them with the additional annual income of about MO necessary to enable them to carry on the work and to keep out of debt. (Applause). INFIRMARIES AND THE INSURANCE ACT. The annual report, after giving the particulars of the working of the institution embodied in the President's speech, stated that the average number of beds occupied (56) was the same as that for 1910, and the average cost per bed amounted to £63 18s 7d., as compared with £63 133 41 on the previous year. The Board, ia common e jib other Boards of institutions of a similar kind in the United Kingdom, viewed with some apprehension the possibly injurious effect of the National Insurance Act upon their finances. They had, during the past year, acted with the British Hospitals Association in endeavouring to obt,in special safeguards for the interests of hospitals, but with small success. In view, however, of the important and indispensable services 'rendered by the Warrington Infirrcary,the Weird trusted that subscribers would not allow any calls upon them under the Insurance Act to affect their contributions, which alone would make it possible to maintaia the Infirmary sea voluntary institution. Mr. W. PETER RyLANDS, in moving the adoption of the report, said the demands upon the Infirmary were, unfortunately, always increasing and if they had the money at their disposal the work done would increase 'by leaps and bounds. They bad passed through A VERY CUITICAL TIME In the history of the Infirmary and they had passed through it in a manner which they might look upon as , highly satisfactory. Five years ago they asked the people of the district to subscribe £16,000 for the enlarged building and it was hardly to be wondered at that when their friends were so generous as to give them that handsome sum, there should be a difficulty in raising the increased subscriptions which were necessary for the maintenance and upkeep of the enlarged build- ing. In spits of that difficulty, however, they had continually increased their sources of revenue until now, although income and expenditure did not absolutely balance, they were getting within measurable distance of it. (Applause.) Since 1907 the subscription list had practically doubled —the amount having increased from £571 to £l,l2o.The donations had more than doubled, the workingmen's suhsariptions had increased about 25 per cent, and the total revenue bad increased from a little over £3,000 to about £4,500 an increase of nearly 50 per cent. (Applause) With regard to the cases dealt with last year there was one INCREASE OF A MOST EXTRAORDINARY CHARACTER for which he had not been able to obtain any seasonable explanation. He referred to the increase of accident oases from 4,400 to 5,500. He did not know whether Warrington men were more reckless last year or exactly 'what it was, but it sounded a most appalling increase. Referring to the outlook, Mr. Rylands said there was a DARK CLOUD imoN THE ItOßlioN in the form of the National Insurance Act and that naturally gave the Board of Management some little anxiety. Of the home patients, he did not think he would he far wrong if he said that not more than 20 per cent would be insured persons within the meaning of the Act. While the Act would undoubtedly provide benefits to those who ware sick and who were Insured persons, and while there were certain provisions of a somewhat nebulous character under which, under certain conditions, hospitals could get some payments In respect of those cases, there was no provision at all under which hospitals could get any assistance in respect of that much larger percentage of cases who Would not enjoy the benefits of the Act. When one considered that the workingraen who at present sub- seribed such a handsome sum as £1,200 or £1,300 a year to the Infirmary would have to pay 3d. or 43. per week under the Act, it was thought possible that they might

S'-TOW SOME DISINCLINATIONN TO PAY the further contribution which they made at the present time. On that point one wished to lay great stress upon the fact that the Infirmary not only supplied workingmen with medical assistance but it also attended to their wives and children who would not come under the Act. Consequently,in spite of the Act the argument whizl induced them to subscribe to the Infirmary to-day would remain practically unaltered and the Board hoped they would continue to subscribe exaotly as they were doing at present. It must be clearly borne in mind that the Act, although it no doubt wbuld do an immense amount of good so far as the field which it was intended to cover was concerned, it did not, by a very long way, cover the field which it was the function of an Infirmary to deal with. 'He hoped tbat when the financial effect of the Act was pressing upon those who generously supported the Infirmary, they would not forget that aspect of the case and that the Act would not made an excuse for the reduction of their contributions. Mr JOHN FAIRCLOCTGII seconded. He pointed out that there was such a demand for beds that patients bad continually to be delayed in their admission for a week or a fortnight. If the Board had more funds they could open another ward. THE OFFICERS. On the proposition of Mr VERE B DAVIFS, seconded by Mr L Wain, the officers were re-elected as follows : —President, Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., C.V.O. ; lion treasurer, Mr Roger C Parr ; hen auditor, Mr C Jackson Holmes ; hon consulting surgeons—Drs E A Fox and G Joseph; hon surgeons, Drs Ernest E Bowden, E J Fox, G 11 Joseph and A Hodgkinson ; hon assistant surgeons, Drs G Binns. C D Law and C Robinson ; bon dental surgeon, Mr H H Taylor. Mr Il Collin was elected a memter of the Board of Management. On the proposition of the PRESIDENT, seconded by Mr IlrNny GREENALL, a special vote of thanks was passed to Mr Geo Dakin for his services as hon treasurer during the last twenty-one years, and general regret was expressed at his resignation. Mr E 3 HALL moved a vote of thanks to the various hon officers and committees who, he said, worked with • great assiduity for the institution of which Warrington was so proud. Mr J FULTON SMITH seconded and the vote was heartily passed. The MAYOR, hi responding for the hen surgeons, said he did not think any hospital of a similar size could be better conducted than the Warrington infirmary.

THANKING THE WORKINGMEN. THE FALLING OFF IN HOSPITAL SUNDAY COLLECTIONS. • The Rev. Joris YONGE proposed a vote of thanks to the Wlarkingmen's Committee for their handsome sub- scription. He said everyone who wrs interested in the Infirmary roust be happily impressed by the result of last year's efforts on the part of the workingmen. It was a rather curious and interesting fact that while they had increased their contributions,they bad reduced their expenses. When they considered the further fact that although they had lost some sources of income,they had contributed considerably more in the bulk, the committee deserved their hearty congratulations. They all felt that the workingmen were doing their fair duty towards the institution. Ile hoped that in the coming year, nothwithstanding what Mr. Rylands had said with regard to the effect of the Insurance Act, they would be able to do as they had done in the past. (Applause.) The REctrort, in seconding, said that of all the grati- fying features conneoted with the hospital, nothing was more satisfactory than the amount contributed by the working Men. Each year their subscriptions exceeded previous efforts, and he wished he could say the same

with legard to the congregational collections, fie did not know what the results of the offettories this year were, but in lOU the total was £240 as compared with £270. _ .

--The Very Rev Father WHITTLE supported. Ile said Canon Willis must remember that the very people to ahom they had ta appf al in church were the working. classes who contributed at the various works. It was difficult ti induce them to give more on Hospital Sunday, especially during the present hard times. The vote was heartily passed. Alderman BURTON, in returning thanks, said he was afraid that the coal etrike which had bsen banging over them like a cloud might to some extent interfere with the Workingmen's Committee's efforts, but he sincerely hoped not. The MAYOR was thanked for the USA of the Council Chamber, on tbe proposition of the Rev. 11. V. PIGOT, seconded by Mr. W. N. llrTctriscs.

The MAYOR proposed a vote of thanks to Sir Gilbert Greenell for presiding. COUNCILLOR ROBERTS AND TilE INSURANCE ACT.

Councillor H. RonERTs, in seconding, said be must join issue in the mildest and gentlest mar ner with the pessimism expresesd by Mr Ryland s with r, gard to the future of the Institution. . . - .

Mr. I.‘YLA:NDSpessimism, I hope. Mr. ROBERTS : I don't know what Mr Rjlanda would call it, but it seemed to be a little bit unnecessarily I gloomy. I for one do not share in any degree what- ever his fears that this Institution is likely to suffer from any want of practical interest on the part of the workingmen. Contirming, he said he did not agree with Mr. 113 lands for a moment that the Insurance Act. imposed additional burdens upon the workingmen but whether it did or not he was quite certain that,whatever the Infirmary might lack in financial support from certain sections of the community, the conduct of the workingmen of Warrington for many years had been such as to justify him in saying that he was perfectly prepared to go bail for them that they would not MI short in their practical interest in the Infirmary, as shown by their subscriptions. (Applause). Mr. J. H. SMETHURST seconded the vote which was passed with enthusiasm. Sir GILBERT GREENALL,in responding,said his duties were considerably lightened by the businesslike help of Mr Hampson.

===== 0002601_19120406_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
LATCHFORD WARD LIBERALS.

CRITICISM OF TORY TACTICS.

MAKING PARTY CAPITAL OUT OF DISTRESS.

At the invitation of their chairman (Mr John Dodds) the members of the Liberal Committee for Latchford Ward were entertained at dinner at Atkinson's Cafd on Saturday evening. In numbers and enthusiasm the workers for the progressive cause in Latchford can hold their own with those of any other part of the constituency and are determined to give a goal account of themselves when the next election comes. Saturday's gathering was largely attended and the greatest enthusiasm prevailed. Mr. John Dodds presided and amongst others present were:—Alderrnan Tinnion, Councillors H Henshall and J T Locker, Messrs P Peacock, J C Holland, E Wynne Jones, Cr Walker, J H Dodds, h New and E A Sanders. After dinner the officers ware elected for the ensuing year as follows:—Chairman, Mr John Dolds ; deputy. chairman, Councillor R Henshall ; secretaries, Messrs John Mather, G Toulmin and E H Jones (Latchford Without). A mast enjoyable musical programme was then submitted. The Orpheus Quartette (Messrs R W Cook, E Walker, W Critchley and L Aukland) sang several , selectiors in a very expressive manner and MrJ W Willett's songs were greatly appreciated. Mr S Aukland delighted the company with his humorous sketches and clever manipulation of the concertina. Mr T F Wallington was an efficient accompanist. The concert was interspersed with speeches. Mr. PETER PEACOCK, as chairman of the Liberal Executive, urged the members of the committee to band themselves together with the objict not only of preparing for the Parliamentary election, tut of increasing the number of progressive representatives of the ward in the Council to the maximum of three. In Ccuncillor Ilenshall they had a tighter and a real worker, and ho hoped the members would encourage him to maintain his official interest in the committee by regularly attending the meetings. (Applause.) Councillor J. T. LOCKER, secretary of the Liberal Association, said be was proud to be associated with Councillor Henshall in municipal work. If ever a gentleman bad brought honour to the Council, particu- larly as a new-comer, it was Councillor Ilenshall, aid he was esteemed by political friends and foes alike. (Applause.) With regard to the Parliamentary election they had in Mr. A. H. Croslield a standard bearer whose interest in the welfare of the town was unquestioned, and wherever Warringtonians went they found the name of Crosfleld honoured. They had an excellent candidate to work for, but even more important was the great cause for which he stood. (Applause.) If Liberalism to-day was not worth fighting for on the part of democracy, he did not know what was good for democracy. He was proud to be associated with a party which was concerned with the troubles and draw- backs of those who were at the bottom of the social ladder and sought to give them a better place in life. (Applause.) lie trusted that, with the assistance of a united band of workers, they would see that at the next opportunity Warrington was not behind in helping to strengthen the position of Liberalism in the House of Commons. (Applause). Councillor BENSHALL said that in the history of modern England there never was a period when Liberals and progressives could be honourably prouder of their cause than to-day. (Applause.) He believed that the flowing tide was with them, The Government were dealing sympathetically and equitably with the new and grave conditions which went to the roots and springs and marrow of the national life. So long as this country was to be governed by party—and it seemed to him that while British nature was what it party government was the system for us—the Liberal party was the one which might be looked to for an , honourable solution of the grave and growing problems of the time. Let them strive to be worthy of a great ' party and a great cause. (Applause). Alderman TINNInas said he wag exceedingly proud of the way in which Mr. Dodds was taking up the work of Liberalism, progress and reform in Latchford Ward. lie was following in the footsteps of his late father who did perhaps more than any other man in Warring- ton to raise the social conditions of the workers and to assist every good cause. (Applause.) As a member of the Town Council he (the speaker) thanked the Latchford Committee for helping to return such a splendid man as Councillor Henshall. (Applause.) When- ever he joined in the discussions he lifted them to a higher plane, while his great business ability and quick perception made him a valuable acquisition to the Council. Alluding to the political sitijatiou Alderman Tinnion said that everybody who considered the present dispute between a section of capital and labour—the mine-owners and the miners—must feel proud that there was such a statesman as Mr. Asquith at the head of affairs. (Applau c.) Not only was Mr. Asquith endowed with very strong character and will power, but he bad a grasp ot the facts of the sitcaticn which must, have been a pleasant surprise to the dis- putants. Ile did not think any man could have con- ducted those delicate negotiations to the same success- ful conclusion as Mr. Asquith. With regard to coming ,1 political issues there was the great Home Rule Bill— that messu e a justice to Ireland which had been too long delayed. (Hear, hear.) There was also the franchise question. Why should one man of wealth who had a motor car be able to travel about the country and register eighteen or twenty votes at one election while the workingman, whose entire stake in existence was centred in the prosperity of the country, bad to be con- tent with one ? livery man should have a right to a fair and equal share in the government of the country. With reference to the political situation locally Alderman Tinnion said ho was amused to read of the deputation which met the Mayor the other day with reference to

===== 0002601_19120406_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
DISTRESS IN WARRINGTON,

- , —wLich should not exist without official sanction. (Laughter). Although he regretted that the Liberal party as a party could not claim credit for the large-heartedness and kindness shown by the ladies and gentlemen associated with the Bswsey Adult School, for their action bad been entirely non-political, he was proud to know that some of the hardest workers were members of the Liberal Party. When they found a great amount of distress in the town, it was a pity that people whose hearts were larger than their sense of otedience should be questioned and practieally censured. It would be a shame if they did not allow their hearts to rule on a question of that kind. (hear, hear.) The Tory Party might try to

MAKE POLITICAL CAPITA': OCT OF THE DISTRESS but the party that was reduced to such means to achieve popularity was in sore straite. Thank goodness the Liberal Party was not reduced to that. they had a noble cause—the betterment of the many and the uplifting of those who through unfortunate circum- stances, could not help themselves. (Applause). Mr. E. WYNNE JONI'S said that if it was only for its past record, the Liberal party deserved the solid back- ing of the working classes. In Mr. Arthur Crosfield— (spplanse)—they bad an excellent candidate, and surely after the experience of the past week they ought to be more determined than ever to turn out the present Member for Warrington. The contents bill of the Tory paper here the words " lladical Trickery " and went on to allege that the Liberals had used their posi- tion to make political capital out of the dirtress. He maintained that that placard was absolutely wrong. If it had described the affair as

"TORY TRICKERY" it would have been much nearer the mark. (Applause.) He supposed that if Mr. New had not written a letter to the Press, they would have beard nothing about the matter. He, however, maintained that Mr New had a perfect right, outside his work as an officer of the Liberal party, to write a letter of that kind and he was sorry for their opponents that they had to take advan-

tage of it. The local Liberal paper had aptly described the deputation to the Mayor as having been promoted by the " peshful Smiths." (Langhter.) At any rate, it consisted very largely of Smiths. (Laughter.) When the Tories had to descend to such methods, it showed that their cause was at a low ebb. If the Liberals' worked with determination, they would win when the next fight came, because they had a just cause and "Thrice armed is he who hath his quarrel just." The Liberal party was doing a splendid work in erecting barriers between the poor and the poorhouse. They made a splendid start with old age pensions—(applause) —and the Insurance Act was another great step in the same direction. (applause.) They had a great social programme, their chief purpose being to brighten the lot of tho people, to make life easier and better for those who had to toil. He heartily echoed the words of Alderman Tianion with reference to the admirable way in which Mr. Asquith had acted during the coal strike. He hoped some means would be found of settling such disputes,without having recourse to the terrible war between capital and labour which they had experienced during the past few weeks. He hoped arbitration courts would be set up. He was not afraid of COMPELSORY ARBITRATION and he could not understand wby those in the labour world should be afraid of it, because be believed strongly that a cause which could not submit itself to arbitration was a very poor one. (Hear, hear.) He was convinced that the Distress Committees set up under the Minimum Wage Bill would go a long way to settle disputes without the arbitrament of the strike. With regard to future legislation, they maintained that in the interests of representative government, of fair play and of justice, it was high time that a measure of self-government should be granted to Ireland. (Applause.) There was a genuine and widespread demand for Home Rule on the part of the Irish people and it was a significant fact that hardly a Tory Viceroy had come back from Ireland without being a convinced believer that the only way to govern Ireland satisfac- torily was to govern it according to Irish ideas. We were on the verge of that question being settled—he believed ()nee and for all. (Applause.) With regard to electoral reform, he contended that the man who once got on the register should be able to take his vote with him wherever he went, instead of being disfranchised when his work took him to another town. Every man should in future count as one man, and no more, at the polling booth. He believed the time was coming when all men would have the right to vote. He was afraid the women would not get it just yet—(laughter and a VOICE: " They don't want it "). He thought there was a great deal of truth in the contention that even among the women themselves, there was no genuine demand for the vote. In future the vote would go to the man not because he had property but because he was a man—and that should be the only qualification for the franchise. (Applause). Mr. Hannv New, in proposing a vote of thanks to Mr. Dodds for his hospitality, said that during the past few days a very grave charge had been launched against the Liberal Party by their friends hi the opposite camp. He wrote a letter to the Press as a private citizen, from his private address, simply because he felt that the need for help was urgent and that, although the meeting of town's representatives decided that there was no immediate cause for relief work to be undertaken, those who had been brought up—should he say with the lower submerged ?—knew full well that poverty was acute at that time. They found the town was not taking steps and, as itaelividuals, they felt the time was ripe when something should be done immediately. The Tories had tsken his letter as a pretext for raising a controversy and trying to bring dishonour and discredit upon the Liberal party in Warrington. Mr. Harold Smith wanted the Mayor ho re,rtet ep au eddi a that a tth eh ework a would r at ihsee Aa dul t e ea a tSecrh- 0r 0 as hea ,p, political e rm,,o vev ee,e fromt e et tehfe its own saeyr vtaot i va et t s eClub.k him ( pre. New) in a most disgusting manner, asserting that he knew tbat something wrong was being done and he waa anxious to associate the Liberal party with it. Yet in the same issue of that paper appeared their own representative's harrowing account of his experiences in districts where distress was acute. The deputation to the Mayor included Mr Fulton Smith. If they walked tip Tanners-lane, however, they would see a poster directing attention to NIESRS. PETER WALKERAND toss' SOUP KITCHEN. He did not know whether anyone would take that as a political dodge but it was peculiar that the Conserva- tives should attack the Liberal party and the Bewsey Adult school in this matter, while Mr Fulton Smith was one of the prime movers in connection with the soup kitchen associated with Messrs Walker's Brewery. What he (Mr New) did was entirely on his own responsibility, and it was non-political. He was a vice-president of an Adult School, and knew something of their work and he believed it could be proved that the majority of those connected with one of the local schools were Conserva- tives. There was no political factor in the Adult School movement. He wrote the letter because he was convinced that there was more distress in the Bewsey district than in any part of Warrington, and that had been conclusively proved by Councillor Toole's figures with reference to the feeding of children. Mr Harold Smith appeared to be determined to gag the Liberal party and he( Mr New) was glad that Councillor Locker, on behalf of the Liberal Association, had -.leen an effective reply, showing that the relief work in Bewsey Ward was no political dodge or trickery at the poor people's expense. Mr New concluded by saying that although he was an official he was not pre- pared to sacrifice his independent views and he would not he driven out of Warrington by the Conservative party. (Applause). Mr. G. WALKER, in seconding, paid a tribute to Councillor Henshall's work as a member of the School Attendance Committee. He was a large-hearted man and took a broad view of applications for exemptions from school attendance. There were cases where children might, in the interests of their parents and to their own advantage, be allowed to leave the school and that redtapeism which would hold a child in school when it was practically doing no good, ought to be swept away. (Hear, hear.) With regard to the distress, as an adult school member he strongly resented the imputation of Nir. Harold Smith and the other Smiths. He knew the individual members who represented the interests of the school in Bewsey Ward, and he knew they were I actuated by those feelings that ought to be predominant in everyone's breast—the desire to help suffering humanity under such exceptional circumstances. They were not actuated in any way by political motives. Ile (Mr Walker) was not at all inclined to condemn Mr New for his action in the matter. Mr New was an adult school member before he came to Warrington,and in his private capacity he had as much right as Mr Harold Smith to diclate when people's pockets should be appealed to in the interests of the distressed. The members of the school were prepared to leave the judgment of their cause entirely in the hands of those who had benefited by their operations and be hoped the work would be continued. Their members would willingly assist in the administration of any public fund, but they would not be dictated to by anyhody as to when they should lift their hands to benefit those around them whom they believed to be suffering. Their action was one of sympathy and of sympathy alone. (Applause . The vote was heartily passed. Mr. Dwells, in returning thanks, said that if they all put their Shoulders to the wheel they could accomplish great things. They might meet with slurs and rebuffs, but their cause was right and just and was bound to succeed in the end. (Applause)*

NEW CHIEF INSPF,C FOR GP CHESHIRE SCHOOLS.

It was announced on Wednesday that Mr GR R Routh had been appointed chief inspector of schools for Cheshire, in succession to Mr F T Howard, appointed to an inspectorship in one of the London divisions. Mr Routh enters on his duties this month. He will be inspector not only for the Cheshire County Council area, but for the county boroughs of Birkenhead, Stockport and Chester and other Cheshire boroughs.

THE BUDGET, favourable comment on Mr. LLOYD GEORGE'S Budget statement is the speech by which Mr, AUSTEN CHAMBERLAIN followed it up. For this reason, we give first place to the views of the EX-CHANCELLOR OF THE EXCHEQUER Mr. CHAMBERLAIN complained that his brilliant successor spoke as if the country were now enjoying a prosperity which it has never:before experienced as a consequence of the Budget of 1909. Tbis, of course, is some- thing that is read into the CHANCELLOR'S speech by an opponent who is clearly gravelled for lack of matter. Then followed the argu- ment to the effect that the CHANCELLOR had a big surplus to dispose of because he had placed last year's estimates unjustifiably low. The answer to that is that it is scarcely possible to place the estimates too low if the policy of the Government is ruining the country, and the raising of an adequate revenue must depend on the adoption of a policy of Tariff Reform—both of which are contentions of Mr. AUSTEN CHAMBERLAIN and his party. A complaint followed about interfering with the operation of the Sinking Fund. Then there was a surprising grievance. This is that the land taxes are not up to Mr. LLOYD GEORGE'S expectations. The former CHANCELLOR OF THE EXCHEQUER wants a commission to inquire into the sub- ject ! And that is really all. Merely carp- ing criticism like this may be passed over without comment. It will certainly not interfere in any way whatsoever with the proposals of the CHANCELLOR OF THE EXCHEQUER. Mr. LLOYD GEORGE towards the conclusion of his speech expressed the hope that when Mr. AUSTEN CHAMBERLAIN is again in control of the financial affairs of the nation he will be able to say that he has raised extra revenue of twelve, eighteen, or twenty-three millions without adding a single penny to the necessaries of life and without interfering with any trade or industry. This is rather an understatement of the case. The great bulk of the payers of income tax have been relieved, and relieved to a very material extent. The men with incomes of over £5,000 a year have been required to make an extra payment to the State, but, as the figures .given by the CHANCELLOR OF THE EXCHEQUER showed, in an extraordinary and most convincing way, they are becoming more and more capable of bearing an additional burden. The total property 'passing at death during the last financial year was 270 millions, and half of that property belonged to 970 persons. That is a fact worth thinking over. The sums left to successors and legatees is in itself prodigious. Those who left something must have numbered many thousands but less than one thousand had owned half the money that was left behind in consequence of 661,000 deaths. Another thing that Mr. LLOYD GEORGE said in concluding his speech was that since the carrying of 'what was described as the ruinous 1909 Budget, employment has gone up by leaps and bounds. This also is an understatement, corrected, however, by another part of the. CHANCELLOR'S speech. There is no recorded time during which unemployment has been so low. It will be interesting to see what Mr. BONAR LAW has to say on this subject. He is now the leader of the Conservative party, and he made his first advance towards that position by saying that what was needed to convince the people of the advan- tage of Tariff Reform was two years of bad trade, or other words to that effect. Mr. LLOYD GEORGE must have been thinking of this when he said that he must be a poor patriot who did not rejoice in the prosperity of his own country lest perchance the avowal of it would do some damage to his party. In the course of debate, there was some expression of disappointment because there

are to be no remissions of taxat and because the CHANCELLOR OF EXCHEQUER has put the whole of his ulg cedented surplus to reserve. There vv°

have been a point in saying that hehad P up a large surplus in order to bribe electors by remissions of taxation, as said about Mr. GLADTONE in similar circl stances in 1874. But, as a matter Of 1 nobody said this because, as was evideal would have been excessively absurd al

the circumstances. Mr. LLOYD G

bribes nobody.

He behaves as a P'

business man would under the same circt stances. His Budget is not to be praised what it does for the taxpayer, rexeept on ground that it gives the taxpayer some sn' security against future liabilities. As

Mr. AUSTEN CHAMBERLAIN'S complaint abc the sinking fund, this one thing at least.fi to be said. No Government that ever e%15 has reduced the National Debt at so greal rate as the present Administration, and 511 the days of WILLIAM PITT no Govern ever raised it so rapidly as did that of party to which Mr. AUSTEN CHAMBERL) belongs.

The average age of cats is fifteen years; of squirrels seven or eight years; of rabbits, seven ; a bear rarely exceeds twenty years ; a wolf, twenty ; a fox, fourteen to sixteen. Lions are comparatively long-lived, instances having been recorded where they reached the age of seventy years. Pigs have been known to live to the age of twenty years,and horses to sixty, but the average age of the 'horse is twenty-five to thirty. Camels sometimes live to the age of 100, and stags are very long-lived. Eagles occasionally, and ravens frequently, reach the age of 100 years, and swans have been known to live 300 years. A tortoise has been known to live 107 years. .

*.* * With the aid of a grant from the Road Board, the Warrington Rural Council have purchased the necessary land for the widen- ing of the road and thee construction of the new bridges at Sankey Bridges. One will be constructed by the L. & N.- N. Railway Co., and the other, the road bridge, by the Lanca- shire County Council. It is expected that the work will be put in hand at an early date, and pushed rapidly forward to com- pletion. Though Warrington Bridge has got a good start, the Sankey bridges will probably be opened first.

* While discussing the adulteration of foods at the Council meeting on Tuesday,the Mayor drew attention to the fact that samples of coffee had been found to contain 66 per cent. of chicory. This was, he pointed out, an important matter for the invalid who might take coffee as a heart stimulant. If it con- tained 66 per cent. of chicory the patient "would be woefully disappointed—if he survived." The presence of boric acid in foods he considered less serious, as the acid was an internal disinfectant—and some people might require one. The question to-day, seems to be : what foods are not adulterated? Would not ignorance be bliss?

Catholic Young Men's Society.

The annual dance in connection with this society will be held in the Parr Hall on Easter Monday. Mr. T. Taylor's band will be in attendance. Dancing will be engaged in frcm 8 to 2.

The Salvation Army The popular Monday night mus'cal meeting proved a success, and was in the bands of the Bewsey Male Voice Choir under the leadership of Mr. Wright, and a most enjoyable evening was spent. Mr. n. Wright gave The fireman's wedding," while solos and selec- tions were well rendered by the choir and friends. Miss Ethel Shaw accompanied at the organ. Sankey Bridges Adult School. Last qunday Mr. Joseph Elwell gave an address on A Defence of Christianity." Christianity, taid Mr. Elwell, " had played a greater part in the process of civiliz etion than any other religion, and notwith- s'anding the criticism to which it had of late years been subjected, the Bible held its own, and contained sufficient to guide the human race through life in this or any other age. GARLAND, 51-55, Bridge at., Warrington.-Advt Liberal Club Bowling. The general meeting of bowlers was held on Wed- nesday. There was was a good attendance, Mr. Joseph Wilkinson being chairman. The vice-presidents handicap will probably take place on Wbit Monday. Several prizes have already been promised. The green is in excellent condition and will be opened to-day (weather permitting).
