The Late Mr. James Duckworth. The funeral took place at Edenfield Parish Church, near Bury, on Monday of the late-Mr. James Duck- wot th, who passed away at his residence, 290, Lovely- lane, Warrington, on the 28th ult.. in his 68th year. The deceased gentleman was a native of Edenfield and in early life was in business with his father and brother as cotton manufacturers in that town. He came to Wart ington about thirty-five years ago and took up a position on the Oka staff of Messrs. Rylands Bros, Ltd. Having served the firm faith- fully for nearly twenty years, he retired and had since lived quietly with his daughter in Lovely-lane.He was a director of the Warrington and District Billposting Co., Ltd., and was very diligent in his attention to the company's affairs. He was held.in high esteem by his co-directors. The funeral was attended by immediate relatives and friends, Mr. A. E. Hare representing the Warrington and District Billposting Co , Ltd. A ff.:mai tribute was sent by the company.

Liberal Ward Committees' Bowling Cup. Gleat enthusiasm is being shown among the members of the Ward Committees of the Liberal Association, at the prospect of having a most enjoy- able time together on the Bank Quay Bowling Green, during the summer months. The competition for the beautiful silver gun presented by the Chairman of the Association, Mr. Peter Peacock, promises to be very keen. Two hundred and seventy-eight entries had been received up to Thursday evening, and as the entries do not close until Saturday next, April 13th, it looks like being a busy season for the Cup C,,mrnittee. The rules are framed so as to enable all to have a sporting chance of winning, whether novice, or in the top flight of bowlers. At the close of the competition for the cup, a gigantic main for all who have entered will take place, and prizes will be given The junior Liberals have als)expressel a wish to take part, and a general meeting will be held on Friday next, when entries will be taken. The Progressive Labour League have also formed a sub-committee with a view to sending in names? The officials and leading men of the party hav,e signified their intention to be in attendance, and mOst of them have entered the competition with the ward members. Mr ' Campbell, the proprietor of the green, will have the green ready about April 20th, for the members, and the competition proper will corqmence as soon as possible after that date. . _ Buffaloes' Military Band. The recently formed military band in connection with the Warrington section of the Royal Antediluvian Order of Buffaloes, held its first public parade on Saturday. Athough there are more than 100 lodges of the Order in Great Britain, this is the first military combination yet formed by any branch of the R.A.0.8.—a great tribute to its Warrington brethren. The bandmaster is Mr. Edward Lawton, who has wotked assiduously to get together a capital combina- tion. Mr. James Woodhead, as secretary pro tern., rendered great service during the time of organization. The first parade consisted of a visit to the six lodges connected with the Order in Warrington. A start was made from the headquarters of the " Coronation " Lodge, King and Queen Hotel, Padgate, at three o'cicck, the route being Padgate-lane, Manchester- road, Church-street, Scotland-road, Winwick-street, to the headquarters of the "Samuel Wakefield" Ledge, Midland Hotel, thence to the "Frederick Bonnell " Lodge, and the "Golden Fleece" Lodge and via Horsemarket-street to the car terminus. The car was boarded for Stockton Heath and the band proceeded to the headquarters of the Victoria " Lodge, at the Mulberry Tree Hotel. After leaving the Square the members marched back to town and the parade ended in Bridge-street, at the headquarters of the " Tom Sangster" Lodge, Royal Oak Hotel, where about 00 members, including the band, sat down to a sub- stantial repast provided by Mr. and Mrs. Sangster. Afterwards an excellent concert took place, in which among others the following took part: Messrs. G. W. Taylor, Pemberton, Bradbury, Gorse and Wolfenden. The chief object of the band when thoroughly equipped will be to promote the benefit of the War- rington District R.A.0.8. Widows and Orphans' Fund.

Death of a Warrington Hremah. The death of Mr. Alexander Flanaghan which took place at hi) home in Jolley-street, on Saturday evening after a seven days illness, removes from the staff of the Warrington Fire Brigade one of its most esteemed members. He contracted a chill whilst engaged at a farm fire at Bold a short time ago and pneumonia supervened. Be had been empl.'yed in the various departments of the Corporation for over 20 years and for the last 16 he had occupied the position cf second engineer in the Fire Brigade. Ile leaves a widow and seven children. The funeral took place at the cemetery on Wednesday. afternoon, Superintendent Turner and all the brigade attending in full uniform. The ceremony was per- formed by the Rev. Father Buggins.—At the meeting of the Town Council on Tuesday evening Alderman Burton (chairman of the Fire Engine Committee), made a sympathetic reference to the death of Mr. Flanaghan and r xptessed condolence with the family in their bereavement.

BOLD STREET WESLEYAN CHURCH.

FOREIGN MISSIONARY ANNIVERSARY.

Good Friday is always taken advantage of by the members of the Bold Street Wesleyan Church as a fitting occasion on which the foreign missionary anniversary may be celebiated. Yesterday morning the body of the church was well filled. The Rev. E Wright Alcock (a returned foreign missionary) occupied the pulpit and delivered an interestieg sermon on the testimony of the ages t 3 Jesus Christ and His gospel. Mrs. A. Booth gave a good interpretation of " I know that my Redeemer liveth." In the evening the annual public meeting was held over which Dr. Ferguson presided. The Rev. H. Williamson presented the annual report which showed that the contributions from the. Warrington Circuit amounted to £306 3 4, an increase of £22 18 8 on the previous year. The majority of the churches had increased their contributions, the most notable of which was Latchford whose contribution was £lO 3 sin advance of that of the previous year. The contributions from Bold Street amounted to £36 14 4, an increase on the previous year of £2 0 3. On Sunday, the collections at Bold Street Wesleyan Church will be devoted to the Mayor's Distress Fund. METHODIST UNITED SERVICE.

The congregations of the Golborne Street Baptist Church and Legh Street Primitive Methodist Church, held a United Service, the collection being devoted to the Mayor's Relief Fund. The Rev. /I. Ogden preached, and Rev. W. Cooper conducted the service.

EVELYN STREET SCHOOL HEAD- MASTER. OUTSIDER APPOINTED.

The School Management Committee held a special meeting at the Education Office on Tuesday afternoon to appoint the headmaster of the boys' department of the new Evelyn- street Council school. Mr. John Upson pre- sided. There were 152 candidates for the position. A special sub-committee was appointed to make a preliminary selection and 14 candidates were brought before the School Management Committee on Tuesday. These included no fewer than nine Warring- ton teachers. The meeting lasted over two hours and in the end the choice lay between Mr. Alexander N. B. Appleton, headmaster of the Prestwood road Council school, Wolver- hampton, and Mr. Sydney W. Jeffery, senior assistant master of Wycliffe School,Warring- ton. Mr. Appleton received a majority of votes and is therefore recommended to the Education Committee for appointment. Mr. Appleton, who is 39 years of age, was trained at Westminster College and is an under- graduate of London University. Ho has held his present appointment at Wolver- hampton since 1903. The commencing salary at Warrington is .£250 a year.

SOCIALIST SOCIETY'S LECTURES.

Large audiences assembled in the Co-operative Hall on Monday, Tuesday, and Wednesday evenings to hear potteries cf scientific lantern Irctures by Mr. Henrylr. Northcote. The meetings were under the auspices of the Warrington Socialist Society. In his first lecture Mr. Northcote dealt with character and circumstance, or heredity acd environment He laid stress upon the " horrible " effect of capitalistic environment on humanity and contended that regenera- tion from without was the only way t 3 produce a healthy and happy human race. The second lecture dealt with revolutiocs cf nature and society and pointed out that scientific philosophy was becoming increasingly cataclysmic or revolutionary. The final lecture was devoted to the subject of man's power over nature and the doom of capitalism. Mr Northcote dealt in a fascinating way with electticity and its ever-increasing application to commercial and domestic needs. By way of startling contrast, he asked his hearers to turn their attention from the wonderful achievements of science to the filthy and degrading slums of our great cities where man's life was reduced to a level to which no wild animal ever sank. Why did we have all that frightful poverty in the midst of so much plenty and of such wonderful r power to satisfy the requirements of every- body? The answer si as that all the good things of life were in the hands of parasites and monopolists, while the great masses of the people were condemned to poverty. The people were, however, at last beginning to realize that slumdum,unemployment and mining horrors were entirely unnecessary. There was absolutely no need for life to be one continuous vale of tears, disease and poverty. All those things simply arose from the fad that the workingclasses were carrying on their backs a load of useless parasites. Under public ownership of the means of life, the wonderful archisvements of man kid the developments of science would no longer be prostituted to the selfish greed of monopolists.

===== 0002601_19120406_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
ANOTHER CHIME I

LADY GREENALL'S GERMAN CLOCKS

THE NOOSE AND THE BOOMERANG.

Her Ladyship has never denied the salient fact that she purchased foreign-made clocks as prizes for her friends at the Dumping Exhibition, but she has hidden, or at least tried to hide, the fact in a maze of verbiage, figures, and fiscal phantasies. For a lady of pronounced sporting tastes she has mani- fested a quite unexpected familiarity with the contents of blue-books, and the intricacies of Tariff Reform. Here she finds Mr. Parker quite at home, and we can safely leave the defence of Free Trade in his hands. We are more interested in some of the "side-lines" which her Ladyship cunningly introduced into the clock question last week. Her latest tu quo que is to the effect that Mr. Parker and ourselves have "cited the very firm (H. Williamson, Ltd.,) from which through a local tradesman, the clocks were obtained." And further that " the cheapest English clock in this ( Williamson's) catalogue is priced at £4 10s. We are supposed to have put our heads into " our own noose," to her Ladyship's extreme gratification. Surely the noose has settled on fairer shoulders ? and if pulled tight will result in a sad tragedy at Walton. H. Williamson Ltd., may have supplied the foreign clocks to the local tradesman. And why not? There is no earthly reason why they should not buy clocks abroad to sell in England. They are not anti-Dumpers ! But where Lady Greenall "gives the show away" most disastrously is in quoting this firm's catalogue to show that "the cheapest English clock . . . is priced as £4 10s." Was that the reason why she bought German clocks worth less than a sovereign each? If so, the blunder was all the more grievous, for was not the Exhibi- tion organised to demonstrate the cheapness and inferiority of foreign goods to those made in England? Lady Greenall must have confused a noose with a boomerang— which we might explain is a purely foreign article that hits back!

Finally, Lady Greenall regrets that quoting from the report of 1911,Mr. Parker should have stopped short at the paragraph which records that the works in BurrEn (Switzerland) of this English "firm were "fully occupied throughout the year." If she will look again at the report in question, she will have better cause for regret The words used by the chairman of H. Williamson Ltd. were:

"I am happy to be able to report a great improvement at our Buren Watch Factory, which

ha; been fully cccupied throughout the year." H. Williamson, Ltd., do not make clocks abroad at all. They manufacture English clocks, which Lady Greenall could have bought for her prizes—at £4 10s. each! To conclude, and to add to her Ladyship s incomplete knowledge on the subject, the clocks she did buy were made by a manu- facturer called Becker, in the Black Forest of Germany, and in the trade they go by the distinctive name of "Black Forest clocks. "

Anything else?

[ro THE EDITOR " EXAMINER."] Sir,—l have read with interest the additional facts which Lady Greenall gives as reasons why she chose clocks —foreign clocks—and how she got these for the Dumping Exhibition. If the reasons are satisfactory to Lady Greenall why should I object? The centro- 'vet sy is of little importance, and is scarcely worth the ,priater's ink that is being spent upon it. Lady Greenall expresses her regret that in giving a ;potation from the report of H. Williamson, Ltd. for 1911, I did not quote some paragraph about works in eit zerland. The report from which I quoted was a summary in a weekly financial paper, and did not contain the paragraph in rjeestion. if I had had .access to the full report, and considered the fact essential to the issue, I should have certainly embodied it. Personally I think Lady Greenall (apart from her position in connection with the Dumping Exhibition) and presuming—a difficult assumption to make—she could not find any appropriate gift of English make, was quite right in buying the cheap German clocks. I sht uld probably have done the same but I am a Ft ee Trader, and in buying a German cicck I would know that my money would not go to pay the wages of a German clock-maker, but through the system of inter- national exchange would go to some British worker in making something which a foreigner, not neces- sarily a German, would take in exchange. I have no wish, as Lady Greenall states, to avoid official figures, but I fail to see the significance of those which Lady Greenall quotes. They would have sign ficance only on the following condition, viz., that it was necessary for our industrial prosperity that we should export equal value of the same articles to the same country from which we'get the identical things. This seems to involve an altogether false ccncep- tion of international ttade, a conception which if carried out would land .this country in poverty. The maximum increment in welfare is secured by the interchange of those commodities which each country can best make. It is tine we import from Germany clocks to the value of £300,000. It is also true that we export to uermany in woollen manufactures, including yarns, £8,833,414 against an import from Germany of £2,231,478; taking the two things togetribr it leaves a balance of £6,601,966 in cur favour, which makes the small imports of clocks look silly. My objection to the proposals for which Lady Greenall stands, is that their effect would be to encourage trades which are not prosperous on their merits, and can only be made so by the consumer having to pay more for what be buys. Protection diverts industry into less economical channels of activity. On the other hand the effect of Free Trade is to cause industries to gravitate towards their most economical conditions. Unprofitable production diminishes the wealth of a nation, and diminishes that wealth amongst the classes who most require it, and who most deserve it. It seems to me folly to endow certain trades with the privilege of becoming pros- perous at the expense of the general community. Protected trades, hothoused by legislation into unnatural prosperity, secure a kind of vested interest by which they prey upon the unprotected consumer. Protection would handicap our export trade through increased cost of production, and cripple our home trade by the general reduction in the real wage as distinct from the nominal, in other words by tie lessening purchasing power, for wages are what wages buy, be it in clocks, bread, or beer. These are some of the reasons which incline Free Traders to adhere resolutely to our present fiscal policy, and it is up t Tariff Reformers if they desire to win over such to protection,to grip these difficulties. In the efforts to convert the electorate by,Tariff Reformers there is no attempt to meet the difficulties, which we, rightly or wrongly, deem to be insurmount- able. To us—we may all be wrong—but to the Free Trader the teaching of the Dump Exhibition was merely playing with the subject. It could be likened to children building a sand wall on the sea shore under the belief they were taking effective means to divert the course of the gulf stream.—Yours faithfully, CEIARLES E. PARKER. Penketh, April 2nd, 1912.

Presentation to Rev. and Mrs. H. Wall.

A large gathering of members of Cockhedge Mission and fiiends, assembled at Bank Quay Station on Tuesday last week to greet the Rev. and Mrs. H. PEACE IN SIGHT.

MINERS' EXECUTIVE ADVISE RETURN TO WORK.

The .Executive of the Miners' Federation met on Thursday to receive the ballot figures, which showed a majority of 42,998 on a total poll of 445,024 against the provi- sional resumption of work. Inasmuch as this did not constitute a two-thirds majority the meeting agreed to recommend a resump- tion of work, but to leave the final decision to a full conference of miners' delegates that will be held in London to-day. Whilst some natural anxiety is being felt as to the outcome of the conference in view of the large majority against resumption,the belief is strong that the recommendation of the Executive will be approved, and that work will be in full swing on Monday and Tuesday. Reports from the districts show that the recommendation is already being acted upon in places.

MAYOR'S RELIEF FUND.

HOW IT IS BEING ADMINISTERED.

The Mayor's fund to relieve the distress now reaches the total of .£Bll.

Any lingering doubt that may have been entertained as to the necessity for opening a town's relief fund must have been dispelled on Saturday. for no sooner did it become known that applications from those who have been reduced to poverty by the industrial dead- lock would receive immediate attention at the Educa- tion office, than crowds of poor people were besieging that building. All Saturday at( erne( n they came in their hundreds, anxious to obtain the where withal to satisfy the hunger of suffering households Under the urgent circumstances close investigation of the cases was impossible, and the officials distributed orders upon tradesmen to ensure that no known necessitous cases should go without food during the week-end. No fewer than 700 people were relieved in this way, and the Mayor authorized help to be extended to many others after making inquiries through the police and other a urces. Altogether a sum of about £BO was expended. On Tuesday morning a systematic system of investi- gation and relief came into force, the Education Offices being constituted the Central Relief Offices Charity workers and others who are appealed to for assistance have been asked to advise all applicants to , go to the Central Relief Offices, where they must give full particulars of their position. Where necessary, tickets are issued (available for one week) for use at the existing soup kitchens, and for children under five and invalids special food orders are given. Throughout the week the officials have been busily engaged investigating the eases as they present themselves and every precaution is taken to ensure that the fund shall not be abused. In order to test the bona- fides of workmen who present themselves, forms are sent to the employers asking for information as to the period of unemployment, works " subs," out of work pay, &c. No relief can be given to persons in receipt of Poor Law relief and where the income exceeds the maximum of 10s. a week tickets will not be issued except in special circumstances. The sub-committee has, however, full discretion in the matter. The persons who have hitherto been supplied at the various works soup kitchens and voluntary kitchens continue to be fed there and the managers, after investigation, have power to recom- mend cases to the Central Offices for additional tickets for children under five and invalids. All new appli- cants who satisfy the committee as to their genuine used are sent to the scup kitchens to obtain food and, when young children and invalids also require feeding, tickets for milk, groceries &c. are allowed. Orders are also made upon the Corporation gas department for the supply of colors. The various soup kitchens will be paid from the Mayor's fund the cost of supplying food to cases remitted from the Central Offices. Messrs. Monks, Hall and Co., Ltd., have Lund their resources so fully taxed in attending to their own workpeople that they are unable to accept outside cases at present. The town relief Las therefore to be administered through the kitchens at the works of Pearson and Knowles' Coal and Iron, Co, Ltd., and the White- cross Co., Ltd. The information given at the Central Offices, is thoroughly sifted by a committee consisting of the Mayor, Councillor H Roberts, the Town Clerk (Mr J Lyon Whittle), the Director of Education (Mr J Moore Murray), the Clerk to the Guardians (Mr A Bottoml,y), and Mr W J Rowland (secretary of the Citizens's Guild of Help) Other officials who have been prominent in work attaching to the administration of the fund are Mr T H Spinks (assistant town clerk), and Mr R B Roberts (assistant director of education). Each works soup kitchen has also an organizing comm'ttee which sits to consider appl;cations. The Citizens' Guild of Help, through its voluntary helpers, is rendering very valuable service in investigating the cases of thglir who are only casual workers. The applications f6r food for children and invalids which have passed through the Central Relief Offices up to the present Wel about 500.

We are asked to annannce that only urgent cases wql be dealt with at the Central Relief Offices between 10.30 and 11.30 a.m. to-day (Saturday) and on Sunday and Monday next.

BEWSEY ADULT SCHOOL

Falls in Line with the -Mayor's Scheme. At a meeting of the Investigation Committee on Thursday, the Mayor read the following letter from Mr. Harold Gray, chairman of the Bewsey Adult School :—" Referring to our telephonic conversation this morning when you informed me that the sub- committee had decided that our best method of assist- ing the relief would be for the Bewsey Adult School to fall in line with the town's system of distribution— that is for the school to be used as a depot for supply- ing applicants approved by your committee - a meet- ing of our workers has been held and it was decided to agree to the above and resolved that every effort be put forth to assist your committee in this neighbour- hood. A complete list is in course of preparation of those who are being supplied here and as soon as it is ready, will be seht to the Town 'Hall. For the guidance 'of your committee, we may mention that we can prepare on our premises sufficient soup fcr 500. The supplies we have been allowing are a gill of soup and half-a-pound of bread per person ; this, of course, excluding children fed by the Education Committee. Children and invalids are receiving milk instead of soup. We have sufficient balance of cash and supplies in hand to enable us to run the kitchen for several days. We intend now, of course, to divert, if possible, ail donations and gifts to the central fund, so that when our present balance is exhausted we will advise you in order that arrange- ments may be made to remit the necessary expense or supplies. Until we receive back from your committee the revised list of applicants to be served by us we will continue, of course. as heretofore. Assn:log you that the members of this school will heartily co operate in working the schemes so that the best results may be obtained." During the past week the kitchen has continued its much-needed and greatly appreciated work of reliev- ing distress in the district which has suffered perhaps more than any other through the strike. The total number of meals provided up to Thursday was 6,128. In addition 432 quarts of milk and 630 buns were distributed. CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETY'S OFFER. Mr. .J. Jarman, secretary of the Co-operative Society, has written the Mayor intimating that his committee have decided to allow £25 per week in shilling crders for goods which can be obtained at any of their branches during the continuance of the present distress. On at:renders received from the local distress fund the managers had been instructed to supply goods to the extent oils. 2d. for every is. of the face value of the order. This was in place of the dividend that would be obtained by members of the society under ordinary conditions and would give to the people the best possible value the society could give without any additional profit coming to the society from that source.

Smith, Captain G. R. Crostield and J. W. Ballen (Hon. Secretary. Plcturedrome Soup Kitchen. THE WATER SUPPLY. Alderman Jchn Evans reported at the Council meet- ing that the Water Committee were in front of the demand for a period as far in advance as July. That state of affairs had been brought about by the intro- duction of a new and economic way of pumping since the scarcity of ccal. The consumption of coal tie been reduced from 45 tons to 25 tons per week. The Electricity Department, Alderman SmErnunsT informed Om Town Council that the electricity department's resources would not. be exhausted for another fortnight, or three weeks. He said a full service of trams would be maintained, and the current for lighting, heating and power for works still continued. There appeared to be every prospect that the strike would speedily terminate, but if the shortage of fuel were prolonged the Water Committee had such a large stock that they could place at the dis- posal of the department 100 tons of coal. Councillor PEMBERToN said it was very nice when one committee had the foresight to provide itself with supplies when everybody else was saying, " There will be no strike "for the chairman of another committee, to suggest that they should relieve his department. If there was a little more foresight in the profit-earning departments of the Corporation, profits would be a lot higher and the rates would be lower. There seemed to be a cheese-paring policy on the part of the Tramways Committee. He (the speaker) did not often advocate the spending of money, but there was such a thing as being penny wise and pound foolish. The state of some of the cars was deplorable. He was in one the other day and was glad to get out of it quickly, as he was afraid it would fall about his ears. (Laughter.) The track also required attention. Councillor HAWTHORN said Councillor Pemberton ought to be sure of his ground before he spoke. They all knew that the favourable position of the water undertaking was due to an economical change which had reduced the consumption of coal. He believed that if the records were looked up, they would find that Councillor Pemberton was one who opposed the change which had brought about the saving. (Laughter.) With regard to the Tramways department, they were looking ahead and hoped they would shortly be able to have an extended tram system. Councillor PEMBERTON : There has been no change at all. We have just the same machinery that we have alwals had. Alderman JOHN EVANS : I am astonished to bear that. Councillor PEMBERTON : The engina is not a new one. It has been down three or four years. Alderman Jolts EVANS : It has not been wolking under the present system. Councillor PEMBERTON : Certainly. -- COKE FOR THE POOR.

Gas Committees' Concession Abused. At a meeting of the Warrington Town Council on Tuesday evening Alderman JOHN EVANS, in moving the confirmation of the minutes of the Gas Committee, said that even with the present economical method of distributing gas, they would only be able to go on with their supply for 16 or 17 days. That was an unfortu- nate position to he in, but they must hope for the best (Hear, hear). Alluding to the ssstem adopted by the committee in allowing small quantities of coke to be sold to poor people, he said that 25 tons were distri- buted in that way on Saturday. But they were afraid that the privilege had been seriously taken advantage of, as the committee had had information that a large quantity of the 25 tons was taken away by certain people, put into carts in the adjoining streets and sold to poor people at a much-increased price. (Cries of "Shame.") Courcillor DICKINSON, as one who suggested that coke should be sold to poor people, thought that instead of the distribution taking place from 9 a.m. to 11.30 a.m., it ought to have been 4 to 6 or 6.30, so as not to keep the children from scbool. Alderman Jolts EVANS thought it was not a great hardship upon men out of work—for whose benefit th 3 coke was supplied—to go for the coke themselves. (Hear, hear.) He did not see how the arrangement which had been in force could have interfered with the attendance at school. (Hear, hear).

THE FEEDING OF CHILDREN. Application to the Local Government Board. _— PRAISE FOR THE TEACHERS. At a meeting of the Warrington Education Committee - on Monday evening, Mr. E. Plinston, chairman of the . School Attendance Committee, moved: "That immediate application be made to the Local Govern- ment Board for their special sanction to the committee incurring the cost of providing food on other than school days for the school children of parents who are out cf employment in consequence of the stoppage of coal supplies." He said that during the past week the number of children fed had greatly increased. The numter of ticket issued was 3,831 and the meals partaken of totalled 2,990. At Wycliffe school 1,168 meals were served, at. Holy Trinity 632, Hamilton-street 518, Sankey Bridges 1256, St Alban's 205, Education Office 181. Ten days ago only 27 children were being provided for. One strange feature was that while 3,861 tickets were issued, only 2,990 meals were provided. Whether that was due to the fact that after the tickets had been applied for, parents clanged their minds be did not know. A fair proposition was, however,. accounted for by the falling off on Sunday. He visited' each centre, along with the Director, the previous day and in ao instance had the number of children expected put in an appearance. Possibly that was due to the fact that, it being Sunday, a special effort was being made to supply food at home. One very pleasing feature of this unfortunate affair was the ready way in which the teachers and the officials at the Education office had given their services. The number of teachers attending on Saturday and Sunday who had promised to look after the children during the Eastor holidays proved that the staff in Warrington were not only sympathetic to the children but loyal to the committee they served. Councillor BIRTLES seconded. The Rev J DAVIES : Do the figures refer to 2,990 children?

Mr PLINSTON No. That is the number of meals

Councillor TOOLE, in supporting, said he visited some of the centres on Sunday and it was a real pleasure to see the interest that the teachers took in the youngsters They saw that all were well satisfied. The CHAIRMAN (Councillor Roberts) : The action of the teachers and of the staff of the Education Office, headed by Mr Murray, has put us all under a debt of obligation. We appreciated this work which is on the top of the somewhat arduous duties attaching to the. office of teacher and to the offices of the staff of tho department. It is extremely gratifying to find the amount of real, solid sympathy which our teachers and staff have in every child sho takes part in the receiving of education in our institutions. I think I am not at all going out of my way when I say we are indebted to. Mr Murray ad his staff for very valuable, assistance In the general scheme which is controlled by the Mayor for the assistance of distressed persons at the present time. There will be a more fitting occasion later on when the present trouble is over—as we hope it speedily will be—to acknowledge again in a suitable manner the very valuable services we are now receiving. The Rev J DAVIES added a few words of gratitude to the teachers and staff, based on his own experience. Lle had spent five or six hours every day during the last I' ten dam trying to do what he could among e small I number, and how Mr, Murray and the staff were able to cover tbo ground he did not know. The resolution was agreed to.

The Warrington P.S.A. Society. A most enjoyable social under the auspices of this society was held on Tuesday evening, in the Cairo- street school. Mr. William Brown presided over a large assembly. Songs were rendered by Miss Hopkins, Mr. Willett, Mr. Pearce and Mr. J. E. Hannan. Mr. G. B. Starkey was the humourist. Mr, J. Foster and Miss G. Starkey were the accompanists. The P.S.A. orchestra, under the direction of Mr. J. E. Hannan,rendered selections.

WARRINGTON TOWN COUNCIL.

A CALL UPON .THE WALKER GIFT 'FUND.

COUNCILLOR PEMBERTON ALLEGES "HIGHLY IMPROPER" PROCEEDINGS.

In addition to the municipal Budget, there were numerons subjects of (iscussion at the monthly meeting of the Warrington Town Council which was held at the Town Halt on Tuesday evening. The Moor presided and there were also preseLt :—The 'deputy mayor (Aldermtn Forshaw), Aldermen A Bennett, T Burton, John Evans, James Evans, W Pierpoint, 3 Smethurst and D Tinnion. Councillors. W H Bellian, T Birtles, .1 Broadhurst, T Chantler, J G Craik, E Cunningtom J Dickenson, J Hawthorn, R Henshall, C Jackron Holmes W H J Jenkins, J T Locker, Dr McKee, 3 Pemberton, 3 Sankey, J Skelland, 0 Toole, J Wilkinson and W J Wilson; Messrs J L Whittle (town clerk), A l' Hellaway (deputy town clerk), TII Spirks (assistant town clerk), J Fairhurst (borough treasurer), W H Woodcock (assistant treasurer), T Longdin (borough surveyor), W S Haddock (gas eagineerl, F V L Mathias (electrical engineer), J M Murray (director of educa- tion), F G Ruddock (borough analyst), Dr J C Ilibbert (medical officer), and 3 Gray %water engineer). NEW SCALE OF PAY FOR THE POLICE. At a meeting of the Watch Committee the Chief Constable produced a report recommending the adop- tion of a new scale of pay for the members of the police force. Ile intimated that, a 3 directed by the committee at their previous meeting. he had interviewed the whole of the members of the force upon the pro- posed increase, and with the exception of one sergeant and 12 constables, all bad expressed their willingness (provided they were granted the increase suggested) to accept a modified pension scale. It was resolved that the new scale of pay as recommended be granted to those who had intimated their readiness to accept the modified pension, which wee adopted. Mr Nicholla was instructed to obtain each man's acceptance of the scale in writing. It was resolved Oat application be made to the Insurance Commissioners for the exemp- tion of the force from the operation of the Insurance Act, the benefits already enjoyed by the police officers being in excess of thoze provided for by the Act. The MAYOR, in moving the adoption of this minute, said the scale was in accordance with opinions arrived at by the Association of the Chief Constables of Great Britain. There was an increase in the rate of pay, but the police would have to serve two years longer before -they were qualified to retire on pension. The DEPUTY MAYOR seconded the motion. In answer to Councillor Pemberton 1he::11von said that all the members of the force had had an opportunity of expressing their views to the committee prior to the adoption of the scale. Only five or six officers objected to the scale—those who were nearing the age of retirement he presumed. Alderman BE cZNETT asked whether the policemen had been consulted with reference to exemption from the Insurance Act, and whether, broadly interpreted, the equivalents offered balanced the whole of the benefits likely to be derived under the Act. The MAYOR replied in the affirmative, remarking that the benefits open to the police were better than those to be obtained under the national scheme, and the men were satisfied. In reply to Councillor Dickenson, The MAYOR said the men who objected to the new scale would be allowed to continue their service under the old conditions and would not be required to go on up to 28 yeang. The Mayor added that the scale was not retrospective, but said that new constables would be compelled to come under the scale. With reference to Alderman Bennett's question, the Watch Committee would have to satisfy the Insurance Commissioners that the provisions already in force were better for the men. Alderman BENNETT . lam glad to bear that, beciuse I think toe Act is enveloped in a good deal of prejudice. Personally, I think there is a good deal more good about it than people are apt to think. Councillor BROADHURST said he hoped the constables who had accepted the new scale would not suffer in any shape or form through their refusal. The MAYOR : No, they will go on just as they are. The minutes were agreed to. WINWICK-ROAD AND LONGFORD BRIDGE. At a meeting of the Paving and Sewerage Committee, the Town Clerk submitted a letter from Messrs John White and Co., with reference to the land required for the proposed widening of Longford Bridge, and the approach thereto,end it was resolved that he be author- ized to negotiate with Messrs John White and Co for the land required for the above improvement ; also resolved that application be made to the Road Board for a grant towards the proposed improvement of Winwick-road and Longford Bridge,and that the Lanca- shire County Council be requested to joie with the Corporation in such application. TOWN PLANNING.

Alderman BENNETr, in moving the confirmation of the minutes of the Street Improvement Committee, alluded to the recent conference at the Town Hall with respect to the Town Planning Act. He said it was well attended, most favourable and friendly in spirit, and augured extremely well for the important proposals which would be submitted to the Council. The MAYOR seconded and the minutes were approved

THE TREATMENT OF CONSUMPTION.

===== 0002601_19120406_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
--- PROPOSED JOINT ACTION BY LANCASHIRE TOWNS.

Alderman JAMES EVANS, in moving the adoption of the minutes of the Health Committee, referred to a conference recently held at St. Helens, to consider the possibility of joint action being taken with reference to the treatment of pulmonary tuberculosis by the boroughs of South Lancashire. He said the conference wes adjourned with a view to laying down some line of procedure under which Warrington, St. Helens and Wigan could use one sanatorium and get the best possible results from the Act. The Mayou, said that by prevention they could do a great deal more than they could ever hope to do by attempting to cure the disease. He did not suppose they would ever find a cure for acute or" galloping consumption, but such cases were not so dangerous to others because they ended fatally before the patient had a chance of propagating the disease. In some cases hereditary influences were so strong that there was no hope of cure. and those cases were a grave danger to others. Dealing with the cases of those consumptive people who had no hereditary or natural tendency to acquire the disease, the llayor said some authorities stated that 70 or SO per cent, were curable. From his own observation ho should say that at least 50 per cent. of such cases were curable. They were a danger to the public,but not such a great danger AS hereditary eases. He could not help thinking that the methods adopted by the Warrington Corporation at the Sankey sanatorium, mainly in the direction of isolation and education, constituted the soundest mode of treat- ment. His experience of sanatoria was that those cases which were curable there, were also curable in other places. A great deal was said at St. Helene to the effect that if they had not sanatoria equipped in the most up-to-date style,the Local Government Board would not give grants to the local authorities. The Sankey sanatorium was just as capable of curing cases of con- sumption and, as far as it went, just as well adapted for preventing the spread of the disease, as any big and stately sanatorium. Mire was no royal road to cure. He did not think there was anything in the way of specialism in the cure of consumption. Ordinary intelligent medical treatment was quite suf9cient for the purpose. Warrington was one of. the pioneers in the provision of local sanatoria and if they could get other authorities to take similar action and make representations to the Local Government Board, they would be exempt from contributing to a big scheme which would only leave a debt upon them. OUR FOOD SUPPLY.

Alderman JAMES EVANS called attention to the annual report of the Public Analyst (Hr. P. G. Ruadock), pointing out that the rate of adulteratton in Warrington bad fallen from 8 i '5 per cent. n 1910 to 506 in 1911. The average amount of fat in rullk in Warrington was appreciably in excess of the general average for the country—a fact which spoke well for i those who supplied the milk, and those whose duty t was to look alter it. It was gratifying to the Council to be able to tell the town that the foodstuffs generally were of good quality and without adulteration, and that not one sample of milk contained preservatives. After some discussion about adulteration of various articles of food by means of boric acid, in the course of which Alderman EVANS said the Health Committee was waiting for the Local Government to lay down a fixed standard of adulteration before taking action, The MAyort pointed out that samples of coffee had been found to contain 66 per cent, of chicory. That was an important matter, especially for invalids. BONUSES FOR PASSING EXAMINATIONS.

The Gas Committee decided that a bonus of £2 should be granted to every clerk who, after attaining the age of 21, succeeded in passing certain specified examinations in commercial subjects. The maximum salary for each class of clerk is not to be extended by virtue of the additional scale, but the possessioo of any of the certificates will be taken into account in consider- ing the transfer of a clerk from a lower to a higher grade. Alderman JOHN EVANS, in moving the adoption of the minutes, said the bonus was offered as an induce- ment to the clerks to better themselves for the duties they had to perform. LOCAL PARLIAMENT. A TIE ON THE BUDGET.

The Warrington Parliamentary Debating Society con- cluded the 1911-I2 session on Tuesday evening when the vote on the Budget introduced by the Liberal Government resulted in a tie. The Opposition amend- ment was lost by the casting vote of the speaker. Mr IlawnionNE (C. E. Belfast) thought there was a wide difference between the budget introduced in the Imperial Parliament and the one they had got there. Which should they accept as the right One? A VOICE : Both.

Ile noticed that in the one they had before them the revenue for 1010-11 was 04,500,000 from excise whereas in 1912-13 which was to be nil. That he understood meant there was to be no tax on beer or tobacco ("No Imperial duty.") If that was so they would have people swimming in drink. (Laughter). Mr. P. DAVRNPORT (C., West Salford) moved the following amendment to the Budget : "That this House is of opinion that the proposals of the Govern- ment entirely fail to face the grave financial problems now confronting thie country and would inflict serious injury upon the industry and commerce of the nation. And further expresses regret that no attempt has been made in the proposed re-adjustment of taxation to give effect to the strongly-expressed desire of our colonies for some system of preferential trade within the Empire Hie task in moving it, he said, wan made harder than it would otherwise have been by the Socialistic ideas the Liberal Party had adopted. He did not agree with the adoption of direct taxation but with indirect taxation. The Socialist members the previous week had been against the taxation of small incomes. The income tax as it was arranged in that budget was al great dis- advantage to the State. The income tax was traditional Liberal !Silence all along. (Hear, hear.) He strongly objected to the death duties, because if they took the money there would be so much less to be divided for the employment of the workingmen. Under the head- of the National Health Bill £1,500,000 was going to be expended. Ile understood that the Government was going to contribute twopence per week for every person insured under the scheme. If so the amount expended in that way would be between three and four millions When they had an income tax so high what were they going to do in case of war ? The fact was that they would not have sufficient money to carry on an undertaking of that sort. There was no .encouragement in the Budget for the development of the products of our colonies. They had taken off the duty on tea and sugar and the breakfast table commodities, instead of devoting their attention to the re-adjustment of the Fiscal system t benefit the colonies whereby they might be encouraged to send us food stuffs. (Hear, hear.) The Canadian at the last election had made it clear on what lines they desired to work. The United States of America offered them their markets but because they had the interests of the empire at heart they refused the often and fought against it. So long as Canada and other members of the Colonies were in competition with countries older than them. selves they had no chance whatever of prospering. It was for the mother country to do all she could to pro- mote enterprise so that all participated in the benefits which such readjustment would bring about. (Applause). Mr E ENGLAND (I. Warrington) said it seemed to him that the Lord Chancellor having been lifted to his high office, bad lost his control of finance. (Hear, hear). The budget he had introduced was a remarkable one. (Hear, hear.) It was an amazing one. What amazed him more than anything else taus that the party he was connected with—the Liberal Party—supported it. Ile would ask those members to say whether they were prepared to vote for a policy which taxed the working- men who only got 15s. per week. Those were the members who had professed themselves to be the friends of the workingmen. If there was anyone who had been disappointed in the legislation of the present government, it was the workingman who found himself worst off than be was fifteen years ago. Could it be the fiscal policy of the present and previous governments were in a great measure answerable for the position in which the workingman found himself to-day? A great proportion of the proposed taxation would come out of the pocket of the workingman They said they would have a surplus of 14.1 millions unaccounted for (ridiculous). That was a most amazing budget and now the Lord Chancellor suggested to the House to adopt a scheme of taxation of incomes irrespective of the sources from which it emanated. The budget was a blundering, plundering policy. The scheme was a most preposterous one. In regard to the extra allowance for old age pensions, it seemed to him the Liberal Party realised that they had to provide for the people they were making poorer by their adherence to Free Trade. It showed distinctly that through the effect of their fiscal policy they had found it necessary to increase the Old Age Pecsions fund by £3,000,000. Councillor HAWTHORNE they are living longer own. (Laughter.) Mr. ENGLAND, continuing, said he saw that the Lord Chancellor with very good intentions proposed to reduce the expenditure on the Navy. The nation could not be expected to go on building year after year again•t a great nation like Germany. It would simply end in ruination. Continuing, Mr. England said he was in favour of the amendment because he believed if they could come to a good business like arrange- ment with the Colonies they could afford to build against Germany and dictate to her our terms,and she would agree to (hens because she would see it was foolish to build against a great nation like ours sup- ported by the colonies. The question of food supplies was a very important one. More than one-half of the food supplies of the country came from ourse.s and the colonies. By a preferential arrangement the whole ef the food supplies would be obtained through the Empire. (A Voice : " Impossible.") It wa3 a well- known fact that they could. (Applause). Mr. MARK HOWARTH (bon. secretary) 4said that first of all he would like to say they had expected the Budget would have received different treatment from the Opposition. It had been said previously that the last Budget had been a trap. Ile defied any sane men to understand what the Conservatives meant by that. A MEMBIER : We did not say that our Budget was a trap. We said the Liberal Party laid the trap for themselves by taking up Home Rule. Continuing Mr. Howaiteer *aid he supposed they meant by the trap that they had been led away by Tories from following sound business finance. The Conservative, easy thriftless way of dealiog with the country's finance was characteristic of the apostles of Tariff Reform. They had heard a great deal from the Opposition objecting to the worker having to pay any income tax. As the Lord Chancellor had pointed out the wo.kers of the country paid 4s 4d per head in food taxation. (Hear, hear). They charged the Free Traders with being food taxers. The tariff reformers said that the tax of 4s 4cl 5V.19 going to be paid by the workers of the country but that the tax they were putting on food stuffs was going to be borne in some mysterious way by the foreigner. If the Tariff Reform proposals should ever become law, would they make any special arrangement, for those workers who were getting less than 255 per week for them not to have to pay the extra tax ? In regard to the amendment he did not think in view of the fact that Lloyd George had a surplus of ,V 3,500,000 there was any particular crisis in finance. It ought to prove to them that Free Trade finance was not quite played out. (Hear, hear.) It compared very favourably with the Germans, their friends were so fond of quoting who had to meet a deficiency year by year. (Hear, hear.) In regard to the second clause of the amendment, he would deny that the colonies as a whole had expressed any desire for preferential trade. The demand had usually come from those who had axes to grind. They

were told that the food supplies and the necessities of life would cost less under Tariff Reform. When the last Conservative Government put a tax on wheat and discovered that the price of bread waa rising and also that a general election was near, they thought the wisest plan was to leave it alone. (Laughter.) If they were going t) make the miller pay more for his wheat then the consumer would have to face the music to the tune of one halfpenny more for a quartern loaf. That would amount to 12s. Gd. a year from a family of five alone. After they had dealt with meat and other commodities there would not be much left out of the 44 4d to be remitted from tea coffee, &c. He looked upon Tariff Reform as the greatest menace to the trades and industries of the country. (Hear, hear.) In conclusion he would say that a great deal of distress in the present crisis had been averted, owing to many firms having been able to obtain supplies of materials from the foreign markets. (Applause.) Alderman BENNETT (C., West Birmingham), said there were many things he liked about tha Budget. He was greatly pleased when last year Mr. F. G. Taylor brought in his proposals for a universal income tax. He awed wits it. (Hear, hear.) He regarded it as a piece of enlightened statesmanship. He was glad to Bee it embodied in that budget that night but be thought 15s. was too low a wage to be taxed and be thought the minimum wage to be levied upon, ought to have been a minimum of 245. to 255. per week, The general principle was however absolutely the soundest thing they could administer. Judging by the experience of the Germans in regard to the nationalization of railways the Lord Chancellor was justified in anticipating a large profit from that source. Supporting the proposal for a free breakfast table, the Alderman said tariff reformers believed in it not sea mere sop to electors or a.s a party cry but as part of one of the greatest and most patriotic policies ever instituted by any party. The tariff reformers proposed to put taxes on various other articles—food included. He did not shirk the question. They wanted to pat a duty on foreign wheat, and there was no reason why they sho old not. They wanted to encourage the British farmer and to give a preference to the Colonies. He did not think it followed that the tax would necessarily ha paid by the consumer, as there would be competing sources of supply. The Colonies had been asking for a preference, at confer- ence after couference, for years. In regard to the Health Bill, he was strongly in favour of the National Insurance Act, and they as a party were strongly in favour of the principle, and one deplored that some of the hazer members of the party had tried to kill the measure. (Applause), Mr E WYNNE JONES (Prime Minister) in supporting the budget said they would notice that practically the whole of the income was to be derived from direct taxation. Every other tax besides income tax could be passed on to the consumer of the commodities proposed to be taxed by the Tariff Reformers, lie believed the principle of direct taxation would get the people to take a deeper interest i 3 the good government of the country. lie believed in the principle of a federated empire and be believed the best way to obtain that was to develop the English langauge and literature and interest in the National welfare. That could not be done by interfering with the business relations between the colonies and other countries. They were told a lot of the Federation in Germany but their friends did not tell them that that federation was on an absolute Free Trade basis. They wanted a complete Free Trade system as in the German States and that was the only way they could get any satisfaction. (Hear, hear.) The mother country had made sufficient sacrifices for the colonies and it was not on the lines suggested by the Conserva- tives that they were going to cement the relationship, but by following the lines of the Free Trade Party. (Hear, hear.) The distances from the colonies to the mother country, the differences in race and communica- tion and various other differences made it impossible for a unity of empire on the lines suggested by the Tariff lieformers. It was impossible to enforce con- formity with their ideas upon the colonies. Such action would make very rapidly for dis-union and dis- ruption. (Applause). The LEADER OF THE OPPOSITION (Hr W H Stansfield said he would like to know what s.ny of the old Liberals would have thought of such a large expenditure in one year. It was a matter of gross mis-management to have such a state of affaire.He believed in 'the principle of a graduated income tax. Hear, hear). The Conservatives believed in a combination of both direct and indirect taxation. They wanted a preferential ps,licy for the colonies. (Hear, hear and applause). On a division a tie resulted on the Budget and the Speaker gave his casting vote against the amendment, which was declared lost. " That being the last meeting of the Session, a hearty vote of thanks was accorded the Speaker and the various other officials for their services which they had rendered so efficiently. Suitable responses were made by the various parties included in the proposition and the SPEAKER proposed a vote of thanks to Mr McDerrnid who had officiated in the capacity as Speaker when he, Mr Ball was unavoidably absent. The proposition was heartily carried.

ROAD IMPROVEMENTS.

At a meeting of the General Purposes Committee the subject of road improvements at Riaton ws revived by a letter from a member of the Council enquiry for the proportional assessable value of the properties occupied by persons who would derive direct benefit from the extensions to the Holly Bush-lane, and by a letter from tha Vie ir of liolliagfare strongly urging upon the Rural D:strict Council the need for alteration to the road at the end of Glazebrook-lane. The Committee and the Council had already taken favourable views of the desirableness of effecting the suggested road improvements. The cost of carrying out the work would be chargeable upon the whole dis- trict of the Council. Hence the reasonable offer of voluntary action in respect of fencing and carting, also reasonable interchanges with owners of land ought to be made by the Council before they arrived at a final decision on the matter. A large sum would bs needed for the improvements. It was decided that Messrs Brookes, Mather and the Surveyor interview the surveyor of the County Cannell on the matter.

MEDICAL OFFICER'S REPORT. Dr. SEPITToN presented his annual report which stated that the birth rate was much lower than last yrar and the lowest they had had for 10 years and equalled 17.6 per 1,000. The death-rate was also lower than last year and equalled 10.7. Small-pox had again been absent during the year. Vaccination had not been as success- fully carried out as in former years on account of the great number of exemptions granted. It had not been found necessary to close any of the schools during the year on account of sickness. Continuing, the report states that a better water supply is required for Rixton- with-Glazebrook. The sewage scheme for Poulton-with- Fearnbead is in hand and is waiting fcr the consent of the Local Government Board. The report was unanimously adopted. VO'l ES OF FRANKS. After the formal business of the Council had been conducted Mr J BALL said that seeing that the meeting was the final meeting for the year he thought it only flt and proper that they should show their appreciation of their worthy chairman by accord;ng him a hearty vote of thanks. Mr. BROOKS seconded and Mr. W. Ft RNESS and Mr. MATHER also spoke. Mr. BACKROUSE in response said the relations between him self and the Council had been of the most pleasant description. He proposed a vote of thanks to Mr Mather the vice-chairman of the Council, who had al ways brought the soundest common sense to bear in everything 11.3 dealt with. (Applause.) A GREAT TREATY OF PEACE. - A vast demonstration in support of Home Rule took place in Dublin on Sunday. Sackville-street ryas thronged from end to end, and addresses were delivered from four platforms. The chief speaker was Mr John Redmond, who raid O'Connell had never assembled a meeting so representative of all Ireland. It was worthy of the occasion, for they were soon to discuss the terms of a greet treaty of peace between Ireland and England and the Empire. The dark cloud of the coal strike had passed away, leaving Mr Asquith svith enhanced reputa- tion and increased power and authority, and they would have a Parliament in College Green sooner than the most sanguine imagined.

TRIUMPH OP TORPEDO BUILDING.

A remarkable aerial torpedo has just be' constructed by the Krupp firm, and is c practical illustration of the powers science. It has been developed frog' , Swedish invention, and is self-propellilio, and without reaction upon the airship aeroplane from which it is discharged. operates somewhat on the principle of„ sky-rocket, being composed of two viz., an explosive head and a tail chauw; containing a slow-burning powder charg,., the gases from which are discharc through tubes at the rear and drive torpedo forward. The initial start is ea, the projectile by means of a launching vice operated by a light powder or e!ectricity. The gas discharge tubes are at an angle, so that the projectile rot 3 on its axis as it moves forward. A qina is oe

sensitive percussion device is used, provided with a safety lock, which -- released when the projectile .has attain, certain speed of rotation. This peculiar vice is so sensitive that it will explode torpedo the instant it strikes an in gas-bag.

===== 0002601_19120406_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
a feather in the balance. The Rev. Henry Braneker, being the Vicar of Padgate, his Orford flock appealed to him to let them have a curate at Orford who should officiate in the school upon a stipend to be found by them. To this request the Vicar lent a ready ear and would have consented but the Rector, the Vicar's patron, refused. There had always from the commencemsnt been a Sunday school at Orford school. It had at first a mere handful of scholars, but as time went on it overflowed the room; and in fine weather many of the classes might be seen standing at their lessons in the open air. Some Warrington ladies assisted in the teaching and also held a sewing class where girls from the factories and workshops resorted on Saturday afternoon to be taught needlework and other household work, especially as much mental arithmetic as would enable them to go marketing. Meanwhile the people of Orford were in sympathy with both the Day and Sunday Schools and Mr. May, the treasurer, built a house for the master at his own expense and Mr. Reynolds, the valued secretary, constantly taught in the Sunday school. In 1852, when the Rev. Alexander Arthur Bri3geman had become Vicar of Padgate he entered into the views of the Orford people and having just then a relative Edward H. Aldridge who had just taken a degree at Cambridge, he offered to present him to the Bishop for ordination as curate of Orford pro- vided the people would furnish the salary which he named and which was to be small, as Mr. Aldridge was also head master of the Grammar School at Downall Green and could only come over and give one full service at Orford each Sunday. The pre- liminaries being settled,the Orford people had to wait until the Bishop held his ordination. Meanwhile Mr. Leyeester having obtained something better, Mr. Thomas England was appointed schoolmaster and clerk at Orford. Mr. England, who was a good scholar, had been a soldier and so a good disciplinarian. He had served 2nd (or the Queen's Royal) Regiment of foot, and had been discharged wounded in the left arm by the same bullet which struck down his commanding officer at the siege of Herat in India. This regi- ment was received by Charles 11. as part of the dowry of his wife Catherine of Braganza. It has the Paschal Lamb as a badge on its helmets, and afterwards when Kirke was its commander, and it was connected with the atrocities at Sedgemoor, the regiment obtained the ironical name of Kirke's Lambs. When James 11. asked Kirke to become a Roman Catholic, the Colonel asked his Majesty to excuse him as he had p-omised the Emperor of Morocco when at Tangiers that if he changed his religion be would become a Mohammedan. _

Farly in this century a man wandered about the streets of Warrington with a large placard on his breast containing in large letters the words "No grumbling." This note might have been necessary had it been brought to Orford while the village was fretting over the loss of the Church for which she had begged the money. (To be continued.)

. GOLF ON THE CONTINENT.

Knock° is the only one, among the many and beauti- ful seaside resorts of the Belgian coast that possesses the natural attractions of shore, country and wood. While building and endowing this new part with all modern comfort, the creators of le Zoute have managed to leave its primitive beauties almost intact. Behind the charrring villas, built on the sea front all the lovely dunes, the length of the Avenues Elizabeth have been prererved. Pretty red-roofed cottages are to be seen here and there, some commanding a view of the country round from the summits of high dunes, others nestling modestly in tiny valleys, encircled in soft green. From the terrace of the golf-club-house, which is perched on the top of the highest dune of the Zcute Estate, the visitor enj ys a magnificent spectacle ! At his feet rpreads the immense plain, where the lovers of golf enjoy splendid sport, he looks down on the Avenue Eliztheth cresting the dunes and e'er elop- ing into a square near the shore, small white pathways bordered with young pollards kind in and cut among the green hillocks, while below, in the distance, tie 63•3 is caught by a group of little white cottages, roofed with red tiles ; it is the hamlet called le Zoute and faired in by-gone days as a den of rovers, law ler s people, quite in& fferent to any progress and living according to their own tenets for many generations ; Further afield again, there lies the open country, through which the Zonte read runs, the favourite walk of Knocke. The receet weather has teen splendid for the golf- course, which is in fine c( niltion and will pro',ably be much appreciated by Easter visitors to this charming sea-side golfing resort. It is raid by those who should know, to be ene-of the best 18-hole courses on the Continent, and has that additional charm for lovers of the be-lth-giving game of being close to the shore, where there is a beautiful beach of fine white sand some fifty yards wide at low-tide, and extending about twenty-five miles along the coast, running by Ostend and Blankenberghe and along to the frontier of Holland, only a short distance from In Zoute-Knocke. Oa log to the fine beach and good bathing, this golfing atation is a great favourite with the children.

"PLAYING TO THE GALLERY."

MR. WALSH ON 111 FOLLY OF CONTINUING THE STRIKE." Mr. Stephen VS'alsh, M.P., addressing a miners' meetinr•. at Wigan OD Monday night, said he had many times known when Lancashire had acted the part of the cat's-paw plucking the chestnut out of the lire for the monkey to feed upon. Scotland, South Wales and Yorkshire were being advised by the miners' leader to go to work pending the fixing of the district rates by the boards; every miner's leader throughout the country with the exception or Lancashire was advising the men to return to the pits, but in Lancashire two or three people were playing to the gallery, swanking, and telling the men to wait until the district boards fixed the rates in Lancashire. There were £4,000 in debt now, and yet the men on the platform were advising them to starve on a little longer, whereas the miners could not get any better terms by waiting thin they could get now in the moment of victory. Practically every county was balloting in favour of returning to work, but in Lancashire, penniless and without funds, the men were being advised to play on and wait for perhaps weeks term making use of their victory. The miners had gained the principle for which they struck.

===== 0002601_19120406_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
COLONEL SYKES'S OPPOSITION TO THE WAGE

BILL.

Speaking at Wilmslow_ on Saturday night, Colonel Sykes, M.P., for the Knutsford division, said the Minimum Wage Bill set a dangerous precedent, because other trades would now say that if this could be decided for the miners it could also be done for them. He voted against the bill because of that, and also because there was nothing in it to prevent future strikes on similar lines. To his mind the bill left open many opportunities for disagreements. A• Government could not fix a wage. The duty of a Government was to govern, and to see that every subject had liberty of actior, and to see that the masters had the liberty to in en their works or mines, and the men bad liberty to go to work and to work without interference. (Hear, bear.) He thought that if this Government bad made it clear at the outset of this dispute that they would carry out the government on those lines, we might have area an end of this unfortunate strike before now.

The Examiner, Saturday, April, 6, 1912.

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

INCREASING RATES.

As anticipated in these columns, Tuesday's meeting of the Town Council disclosed arrangements fr a ratbc r formidable advance of the rates, the provision in respect of education accounting for a substantial part of the increase. Education, of course, is a national nccessily, and I think it ought to be supported absolutely by grant from the National Exchequer. Some will at once jump to the conclusion that that would come to precisely the same thing in the end ; but a moment's theught should suffice to con- vince these that taxation wholly for such purposes by the State would mean that only those who really had ability so to do would be required to contribum. It seems to me that if that principle were substitu ed, a town like Warrington would be relieved to the extent of 2s. in the £at least. Why should the administra- tion of the Poor Law be put upon the local authority as it is? Surely that, too, should be a charge upon the State. I can understand the borough having to maintain its own roads and attend to such matters as sewerage and sewage disposal, because it controls these through its elected representatives, and receives fr.in them benefits for which it ought to pay. But it ought to have much greater financial aid in relation to departments the efficiency of which con cerra the entire country and regarding which the central anther ities claim the right constantly to inter- pose. lam disposed to agree that the disproportion between local and national administration will never be altered until we send to Par!iament fewer lawyers and more business men. However—as the reader has by this time discovered—l am but a child in these matters.

THE FOOD QUESTION. I feel much more at home in chatting about some- thing to eat, ard the incidental allusion to the Poor Law suggests a few remarks vith reference to the Rev. A. M. Mitchell's attitude as a vegetari tn to the !peat pork question. I share with another writer

Health week which Warrington is presently to observe in common with many towns will show unto as a more excellent way. I suppose that towards the end of the month ministers will preach on health, teachers in the schools will give their children some instruction on it, and all concerned for this greatest national asset will let us have the benefit of their advice and counsel. Among these the vegetarians will have a place ; Liter all, they have a mes4age, and it is well to incline an ear that way. I once attended a banquet in connec-• tion with the cult, and, to be quite frank, I should not like to undergo such another ordeal ; still, they have some capital recipes, and many other d‘shes go well with a mixed dietary. lam told th t about 40 different Councils have adopted the health week. The Mayor of one of these boroughs recently stated that he had applied for the en-operation of the cinemato- graph managers, and had been assured of their best support in putting before the public films having a bearing upon health. Another copimi tee that was to be formed (he went on) was a municipal works visiting committee. There were many who only saw the Town Hall twice a sear, when they paid the rates. They did not realise the great undertakings which the Corporatim was responsible for. It was intended to throw open the health, electricity, tramway and other departments. A ladies' committee would Els° be formed to organise Morris dances, exhibitions of physical drill, children's sports, voluntary society visits, such as the creche, &c. There was a big possibility of great interest being shown during the health week. It was not proposed to have any con- troverial subjects dealt with. Everything would be presented in as plain and as simple a manner as possible. That's the style. Now Warrington, buck up! GOOD FRIDAY.

Why " good ?" Surely in tLo serse of " holy " since it commemorates the world's supremo tragedy, the day on wh;ch "those blessed feet . . . . were nailed for our advantage to the bitter cross." As one journalist comment—That " bitter cross" is imprinted en the little bits of bawl called hot cross buns which we still eat on the morning of Good Friday, just as we reserve our symbolical " Pasch " or '' Pace," eggs for the following Sunday. invariable sorrow for those who deem it necessary to punish 'themselves continually for to better cause than the mere fact of being alive, and after the lapse into whit h the rev, gentlemen was betrayed at last 11 week's meeting cf the Board of Guardians, I think it may fairly be assumed that Mr. Mitchell's asceticism is by no means of that extreme order. One vegetarian who—if the convenient phrase be not cffecsive in this connection goes the whole hog, pressed into my unwilling hands a bundle of literature with solemn warnings against the consumption of butcher's meat, and special emphasis uren a namphlet with the ominous title " Perils of pork." I asked this earnest apostle if, seeing thit his conversion dated but a few short years back, ho had not sometimes forcibly to suppress a yearning for the fleshpots—if, for instance, on passing an cren door and involuntarily sniffing a joint in precess of roasting, be did not at least for a moment pause in his wilderness journey and commune witl in himself as to the desirability of a return, if only on a sort cf po? visit, to Egypt? He simply snapped out something to the effect that these things were an abomination unto him, turned sharply on his heel, and hurried away. He happens to be a neighbour of mine, and since then I have seen the butcher's boy at the door. What could be the meaning of that? Is it possible that it is a case similar to the following, which I find in the current "Punch's Almanack " : Young Lady : " Well, Mrs. Higginbottom, and how are you getting on? Won't you have another piece of cake ?" Old Woman (with an eye to the ham sandwiches) : " Well, mum, if it's all the same to you, I'd rather 'aye a taste of summat as has drored breath ! " Food reformers of Mr. Mitchell's persuasion are accustomed to putting considerable reliance on the Asiatic argument; but I remember once reading in "The Hospital" that, so far from being a vegetarian, as popularly supposed, the Chinaman living in parts remote from the coast, and hence removed from the influence of Western ideas, consumes p ,rk, game, mutton, dog's flesh, goat's flesh, and eggs in abund- ance. These last in an advanced state of decomposi- tion constitute a very recherche dish. In qanton, dogs are fattened for the butcher, and throughout the empire fish is a staple d sh. It is no more true to say that a Chinaman lives entirely on rice than to main tam n that an Irishman lives exclusively on potatoes- FADDISTS.

The increasing intricacy of the food problem is my excuse for continuing the subject. I use the term in deference to public opinion. For myself there is no problem at all. A medical man solved it for me about a quarter of a century ago. Said he "As a rule, what you fancy agrees with you." I know not how it may be IN ith others, hut that has worked out all right in my experience. I am not moved by the persistent tub- thumping of extremists on one side or the other. A I while ago, I had occasion to call at a certain surgery on business, and I found the doctor munching standard bread. He handed me a chunk, and we discussed its merits. He was full of it—l don't mean of the bleed, you know; and he was greatly scandalised, not to say disgusted, when I produced a copy of "The Medical Magazine" containing an article which was quite severe on oatmeal, brown bread, " standard " and other "aids to digestion." Oatmeal, it was stated, "although indulged in with impunity by large numbers of Scotsmen, has long leen known to be liable on occasions to cause ulcera- tion and obstruction on account of the irritating character of the silicated busks. The effect, indeed, I must be somewhat similar to that produced by swallowing powdered flints or glass.' The same applies to brown bread, wholemeal bread, etc., while for even the much-vaunted "standard," cent titling as it does a certain proportion of powdered bran, an unusually large amount of cellulose and of mineral constituents, cannot be considered free from the same element f danger. Thus the medical organ. What have the members of our Caledonian Society to say to this? I presume that some of them have been reared on this powdered flints and glass diet. True their immunity from dire consequences is taken for granted. I have taken upon me to trtat of food faddists, whose name is legion, and I am reminded by "Good Housekeeping "that there are among us people pledged to eat only uncooked food. We are seriously assured that these raw fooders never have colds, fevers, stomach trouble, torpid livers, nervousness, or a lot of common, everyday ailments that the out-of-date, ordinary cooked-food eater has. But raw toad ! To the uninitiated it does indeed sound brutal. The explanation, of course, is that the uncooked food devotees eat no flesh. I set out with the intention of indicating what they do eat, but on second thoughts I pray the interested reader to have me excused—it is altogether too weird. THE PRt.POSED HEALTH WEEK. The vegetarian, or ftuitarian, or whatever other name he may prefer, finds in total abstinence from butcher's meat the panacea for all the ills that flesh is heir to; tut doubtless the promoters of the National

surerstition regardiu g bread baked on Gocd Friday appears to have existcd from early times. Bread SJ baked was kept by a family all through the ensuing year under the belief that a few gratings in water would prove a specific fcr any ailment, more parti- cularly for diarrhcca, and of this superstition a memorial may be seen in our hot cross buns.
