That is to-morrow. St. Paul sums up the import- ance of the rnsuir ction in a few terse words, "If Christ be not ris3n then is our f dth vain." Hence it is that Easter is the greatest ttf all the festivals of the Church. The truth of Christianity rests on the event it celebrat)s. There is, however, another reas for the importance of the Springtido festival. Spring has been always the chief season of joy and festivity. EASTER ASSOCIATIONS.

Easter is particularly rich in legendary lore and traditional customs. I have given one quotation respecting the bun. Here is another Dom a friend of my own :—When you eat your hot-moss bun you I do not think you are doing very much the same thing that your savage ancestor did in honour of a pagan go 4 more than 2,000 years ago, and are taking Fart in a custom which has come down from that time. Yet so it is, if we are to believe those who ought to know. The early Christian priests did not set themselves to stamp out deeply-rooted customs which were not harm- ful in themselves. They did not rurs counter to national prejudices. They probably knew that such acts would on'y alienate those they wished to rlacate, and rouse a storm of hostility where they desired peace and 'fricndship. The priests took what they found, and adapted it to their7own principles, and made heathen customs into Chi istian ones. Thus the putting out of fires at the season became symbolic of the dark- ness of Calvary; the relighting of the fire at Easter became symbolic of the resurrection, Stc. Similarly the cakes eaten at Easter in honour of heathen deities were hallowed by being marked with the sign of the cross, and became the hot-cross buns which are still a feature of Easter. NUTTY.

One day this week, I fell in with a Wesleyan minister, and he palmed this little yarn on my guile- ' less self : When I was stationed in Scotland, an American tourist who was " doing" the Highlands asked an old shepherd to guide him over the district constituting my circuit. 'The two talked ab-ut the weather, and the Scotsman said, " Ah, but it's cauld up here i' the winter, moo, ye ken naught aboot sic like waur ye coons frae ! " " That's whar ye make your mistake," said the Yankee. " Why er 311'11 b'liere me, I seen a sheep jump hum a hill, an' it wuz so cold that it fruz her whar she wuz, an' just left her hanging right thar in the air!" "I canna believe that," said the shepherd, " cos the law o' gravitation wudna allow o't." " But," explained the visitor, ye see in those parts the law o' gravitation wnz him, too!" I looked narrowly at the narrator, and he withstood my scrutiny. THE GREAT INSURANCE ACT,

SICKNESS BENEFIT.

BY C. A. McCURDY, M.P.

The Act provides for all insured persons, in case of sickness: free doctoring, free drugs, free medical and surgical appliances, so far as may be found necessary by the Commis- sioners, and a weekly payment during sick- ness, which varies according to the age of the insured person. Let us take, as an example, the man aged from twenty-one to fifty, repre- senting, as he does, by far the largest class of the community which will derive benefits under the Act. In cases of sickness he will receive for, twenty-six weeks, if the sickness lasts so long, a payment of 10s. per week. If lie should become prmanently disabled when the twenty-six weeks come to an end he will receive a further payment of ss. a week for the rest of his life, or until he is qualified to receive the same amount under the Old-Age Pensions Act. In addition to these benefits, he is entitled, if attacked by consumption, to free treatment in one of the sanatoria which will be provided by the State. While lie is being looked after by the State and his disease being cured, his wife and children will get the sick pay. We must not overlook the fact that the Act also makes provi- sion for the payment of 30s. to every in- sured person whenever a child is born. If the mother is herself employed and insured under the Act, she will get a sick benefit of 7s. 6d. a week for four weeks during her confinement, which brings the maternity benefit in such cases up to £3.

===== 0002601_19120406_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CONTRIBUTIONS.

What is it that the working man will pay as a contribution? In the case we are deal- ing with, of a man between twenty-one and fifty, what he pays will depend upon what he earns. If his average weekly wage does not exceed 9s. he will pay nothing at all. If his wage exceeds 9s. but does not exceed 125., he will pay a penny a week. If his wage ex- ceeds 12s. but does not exceed 155., be will pay 3d., and those whose wages are over 15s. a week will pay 4d. The actual cost of the benefits varies ac- cording to the age of the contributor. The youth of sixteen gets under the Act nearly double value for what he pays. The man of thirty-five gets more than double the value of what he pays; whilst in the case of the man of forty-five or older the benefits are far in excess of the contribution which Ile is called upon to make.

===== 0002601_19120406_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
VALUE FOR MONEY.

The Registrar-General of Friendly Socie- ties has calculated that in the case of collect- ing societies in this country 2d. out of every 4d. collected goes to management expenses. II his calculation is accurate the worker gets two pennyworth of insurance for every 4d. he subscribes. Under the Act he gets, for a contribution which ranges from nothing to 4d. a week, benefits which, between the ages of sixteen and forty-five, vary in value from 7d. to 11d. How can these benefits be given for the small weekly contribution provided for by the Act? The answer is simple. In the first place, the cost of collection is saved by the affixing of stamps to a card instead of em- ploying a collector. In the second place, none of the expenses of the Central Board of Management are charged to the Insurance Fund, and the approved societies which will administer the benefits are debarred from being profit - making companies. Not one penny of the workers' contributions can be used In payment of profits to other people, or in paying wages for collection. For every 4d. the worker pays he gets four- pennyworth of insurance. In fact, as I have shown, he gets far more than four- pennyworth for every 4d. that he pays. The difference is accounted for by the fact that to every 4d. which the worker contributes under the Act his employer and the State have between them to contribute another sd. or its equivalent. In the case of a worker whose wages fall below the average rate of 15s. a week his weekly payment will be less than 4d., but he gets the full insurance money just the same. He pays less because he can afford less; the employer pays a higher contribution because he is at present getting the advantage of cheap labour, and can afford to pay for it.

===== 0002601_19120406_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
NONE TOO OLD TO INS-Ulu.

When a man has passed the age of fifty few friendly societies will welcome him as a member; if they do, he finds the contribution necessary at that age much higher than in the case of a younger life. Under the Act those who commence contributions over the age cif fifty and under sixty pay the same contribu- tions as if they were sixteen, but receive a reduced money benefit of 7s. for men and Gs. for women, and if over sixty on entry 6s. for the first thirteen weeks and ss. after- wards for both men and women. The Act gives those who have neglected to join a friendly society an opportunity of acquiring substantial benefits which no friendly society could afford to give them. Unmarried boys and girls until the age of twenty-one get a junior scale of benefits. For boys, Gs. for thirteen weeks and ss. after- wards; for girls, ss. for thirteen weeks and 4s. after. But if an inspred person, though under the age of twenty-one, is married or has dependants to support, he or she is entitled to the full rate of money benefit. HOW ARREARS ARE TREATED UNDER

===== 0002601_19120406_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ACT.

There is no necessity to keep up subscrip- tions during sickness or unemployment under the Act. No arrears can be incurred during sickness, and if ss member falls into arrears owing to unemployment his approved society can excuse part of the payment if it thinks fit. If his society does not excuse payment he is allowed three weeks' free arrears for every year that he has been insured, and if he is in arrears to a greater extent is still entitled to a sub- stantial scale of reduced benefits under the Act. All arrears are counted only on the average of years over whicli. the contributor has been insured, and no arrears are counted for the first year. A CASE OF ARREARS,

Suppose at the end of two years a con- tributor has thirty-eight weeks' contributions unpaid—twenty-six weeks the first year and twelve weeks for the second. Whether his first year's lapses were due to sickness or unem- ployment does not matter; they do not count at all. Of the twelve weeks in the second year, assume he was ill six weeks. They do not count either. That leaves six weeks only that count, and those must be averaged over the two years, and as he gets three weeks' free arrears for every year that he has been insured he is not, for the purposes of the Act, in arrears at all. No one can fairly say that that is not generous treatment; but the policy of the Government is to make it diffi- cult to get into arrears, so that no benefits may be lost.

===== 0002601_19120406_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
ANOTHER CASE.

Suppose that at the end of two years, after making every allowance for sickness, and for the first year he had been nine weeks in arrear—only three weeks would count, and he would be entitled to 9s. 6d. a week sick pay, and every other benefit under the Act in full; and in twelve months, without pay- ing up a farthing, those arrears would be automatically wiped off and he would be in full benefit again. Sickness benefit is not lost until a man has thirteen contributions unpaid, on the aver- age; for every year—exclusive of arrears in- curred during sickness and during the first year. Once insured under the Act it will be, in practice, impossible for a steady man to lose his right to benefit.

SOME CATCHES.

Boys and girls are fond of catches (I know this because I have been caught myself so often) and so I am going to make them a present of one or two which I have heard lately. I have purposely kept them back until after the Ist of April. A young lady was once talking with a very young and very smart man, who was inclined to air his knowledge of the languages a little beyond what sho felt modesty required. She therefore said to him with an air of deference to his superior attainments : "You are a Latin scholar. I wish you would tell me how to pronounce the word so-met-i-mes."

The youth with a kindly air of patronage, replied, "I have not met the word in my Latin reading, but I should have no hesita- tion in saying that it should be pronounced So-met-i-mes" (giving it four syllables, the accent on the second). "Thank you for telling me," replied the girl demurely. "I have always heard it pronounced "sometimes," but if you say the other way that must be right." This is similar to the catch of " bac-kac- he," which turns out to be backache. It also reminds me of a question printed some years since, as to the way of spelling " need "—to need bread. The average person will reply " k-n-e-a-d,' of course," but the answer will be that we " knead dough, and need bread." A young lady recently misled a family in a most heartless way. She remarked, "I had a letter to-day, and how do you think the little preposition 'to ' was spelled I" "Too," suggested mama. "Two," suggested papa. " Tew," " Teu," " Tu," ventured various

voices.

"All wrong," exclaimed the young lady, when the alphabet and ingenuity were all exhausted.

Just then Teddy, who had been soberly absorbed in his bread and jam, and who was in his first term of school, and wrestling with the problem of words with two letters, raised his head, and with an air of decision and importance, gravely spelled " T-o, to." "Yes," cried the young lady with a laugh. "Why," exclaimed the others in dismayed chorus, "that is tin right way to spell it ! " "Exactly," she replied, "and that is the

, way my correspondent spelled it. You do not suppose I correspond with persons who cannot even spell the word correctly, do you ? "

NOT BRILLIANT.

Bobby's father is not a brilliant sort of man; it has been said, in fact, that he errs decidedly in the other direction.

He towok Bobby the other day on a short visit to some friends, and, thinking to make a good impression, he started a conversation. "I had quite an adventure the other even- ing," he said. "At the corner of a dark street a man suddenly spr‘ng out on me, and said that if I didn't give him my watch he'd knock my brains out. Fortunately a police- man came along, and the than took to his heels ! "

"And weren't you afraid ? " asked his hostess. " Afraid ! " echoed Bobby's papa modestly. "Well, I hope not." "But," went on the lady, "if he had carried out his threat ? "

" Well," answered the visitor, "if be had --" And then the loyal little Bobby struck in.

"I'll bet tiler's nobody who could knock any brains out of father!" he said proudly.

A BID FOR A BRIDE

BY BLANCHE EARDLEY, Author of " Kitty Bell—Actress," "The Lady

Killer," &c.

===== 0002601_19120406_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAPTER XVIII%

XEE MAN WITH THE SCAR.

In the pretty little Devonshire town of Paignton, Mr. Wellington Scafe was enjoying himself hugely. The sea was delightful, and he used to sit on the pier sometimes and shudder at the thought of returning to Scot- land Yard and his duties there. But he had almost completed his inquiries- in Paignton, and now, seated in the shade of one of the many covered little shelters on the front, he took out his note book and glanced through it thoughtfully, conning the items to himself in an audible voice. "A Mrs. Ford and her baby girl stayed at No. ID, Freeman-street, for two years. She wads a widow and lived a quiet life, seeing no -visitors and going nowhere, and telling no one about herself." He turned the page and went on in the same monotone. "Once a man called to see Mrs. Ford, a tall dark man. He stayed three days in the place, and it was noticed that Mrs. Ford was very sad for some time afterwards, and from that time she wore black." _ _ _

Then he took out of the pocket of the book a folded sheet of paper. It wao a birth cer- tificate, and stated that a female child had been christened at the church of St. John, on December the sixth, eighteen eighty-eight, and the name of the child was Carlotta" Mr. Scare folded the paper and put it back into the pocket book, then he sat and gazed out to the oea.

"Then the girl should be about nineteen if alive," he muttered. "I wonder if she is. I should very much like to know what became of her eventually. The mother left these parts when the child was two years old and left no trace behind her, it seems. I'm afraid I shall have some difficulty in tracing her— if I ever do. It's a pity all those letters were stolen from Mr. Tennant; it would have been easier then to discover the mystenious Mrs. Ford, whose name might be anything." ••

Presently he rose and strolled along the pier, and then, after having exhausted the at- tractions to be found there, he left it for for the now almost solitary beach. He had struck up an acquaintance with an old fisher- man who had a cottage close by, and his steps took him that way almost unconsciously. The old man, whose name was Peter Crane, was about the oldest inhabitant of Paignton, and though he cultivated the old "salt" for his own sake, Mr. Scafe also had an eye to his business.

As he neared the cottage, Peter Crane came out and greeted him cheerfully. "A fine day, sir." "Yee. I shall be sorry to leave your plea- sant little watering place," the detective said slowly. "London will seem very ugly by comparison." The old salt nodded sympathetically. "Ay, that's true, sir; them as comes to these parts don't want to leave them in a hurry. May be you'll be coming back? " "I don't know, but if ever I do, Peter, I will come and see you. I suppose you have lived here all your life? " "Nearly all, sir," the old man answered, "but in that time I've seen a few strange things happen even in these parts!" "Anything exciting?" the detective said, taking out his cigar ease and handing it to his old companion. "Some yes, and some not; but one thing stands out as clear as clear, even now after all those years." "What was that? A wreck? " queried Mr. Scare carelessly. "No, not a wreck, sir, but an accident to a private party that at the time I was inclined to believe was meant to be a murder," the old gossip answered. The light sprang suddenly into the detec- tive's eyes. "A murder!" he exclaimed, "how did it happen? " "It was nigh on twenty years ago now, sir, but I can recollect it as plain as plain even now. I was in charge of some boats on the beach, and one day in the summer a gentle- man and lady hired one of the boats from me, and the gentleman refused to let me go out with them, as he said he could manage it. him- self. About a couple of hours afterwards I grew anxious about them, when the boat didn't turn up, and set out in another boat in search of them." He paused and wiped his mouth reflectively. "Well, I found the boat lying keel upwards, and quite close by on some rocks that the tide was quickly cover- ing I found the lady unconscious—but the gentleman had vanished." The detective stared at him wonderingly. "Vanished? How on earth do you make that out?" "I don't know, sir; I only know that tho gentleman was not to be seen, and the lady and the boat had been abandoned. To my mind it looked as though it had been done a purpose. The rocks were slippy, and she might 'a fallen; but it was a blow that had knocked her down, and it struck me that the gentleman had meant to kill her." Mr. Scafe was silent. In the old sailor's story he saw a sudden possibility in connec- tion with his own quest. "I suppose it's so long ago that you don't remember what the couple were like?" he said presently. "The lady was very young and fair, and the gentleman was tall and dark. But I'm wasting your time, sir," he went on. "I can't leave off when once I begin." "I am very much interested," the detective said quickly. "Do you know if the gentleman was ever found?" The old man shook his head. "No, sir, I don't; his body was never washed ashore these parts, and I've allus 'ad strong ideas as he was never drownded! The poor young lady got better, but to all questions she would say she couldn't remember,' and shortly after she left the place, and the whole thing was forgotten. I've not talked about it since, sir." Mr. Scafe drew a coin from his pocket. "You must be thirsty after our long conver- sation, and I should like a little mild refresh- ment myself. Can we get anything about here?" "If you'll accept what I've got in my little place, sir, you're welcome; there's not much, but what there is is all right." As they entered the cottage Mr. Scare put down the coin on the table, where the old man would find it after he had gone, and then, seeing he was gazing wistfully at the paper that was sticking out of his pocket, he drew it out and handed it to him. "This is the 'London Daily Reflector.' Would you care to have it? It is illustrated." The old man seized on it eagerly, "Thank you, sir. I don't often get tho London papers." As Mr. Scafe turned to pick up his drink, he heard an excited cry come from his host, who was gazing at the inner sheet of the paper with amazement in his weather-beaten old face. "What is it?" the detective said. "Found a missing relation?" "I don't know, sir. I'm fairly moithered and puzzled, but if this 'ere chap isn't the living image of him what we was just talkin' about a few moments ago, I could eat my 'at." The detective looked over his shoulder. There was a group of illustrations, and he could see no particular one that could have reminded the old man of his conversation. "There, in the left 'and corner," said the other excitedly; "it's the living image of what that gentleman might have become if 'e was not supposed to be drownded!" The detective glanced at the picture to which the grimy forefinger was pointing, and saw to his surprise that it was no less a per- sonage than the financier Clifford Hawke. !'Whv—that's a great City man." Mr. Scafe

"Well," the young nfarf said "quickly, "what news, Scafe? Any hope of tracing that chap?" "I don't think so.- At least from what I can gather I can hardly hold out hope yet, but of course one never knows. I learnt that a lady called Mrs. Ford and her baby stayed at Paignton for two years, and that she was supposed to be a widow, and only once had a visitor—a tall, dark gentleman— and after that visit she was very sad, and soon after left the place with the little baby, whose name was Carlotta."

"Poor girl!" Tennant sighed. "I suppose she had bad times to face when that scoun- drel left her! It seems hopeless to go on with the quest," he added; "all ray clues are gone now.

Mr. Scafe's keen eyes glanced curiously at the pale, bitter face of his employer. Though it was some time since the mysteri- ous attack on him in the East End, he still seemed to be lacking in the high animal spirits that had been so evident in the days before then, and the detective knew enough of human nature to be able to define the reason of the change. Only a man disap- pointed in love could look as Jaspar Tennant did, and Mr. Scafe wondered how it was that any girl could have played him false. "If I were you I would not drop it just yet," he said. "It is early clays to give up, and there are at least one or two things made clear."

Jaspar looked at him in surprise. "What are they?" "The first is that Mrs. Ford, your sister, was alive when she left Paignton, and. the second is that she was once visited by a tall, dark man who mysteriously disappeared." "I don't see that that is much help," Jas- par replied, "but you may go on if you think it any use. There was one man who could have helped us, as from my sister's let- ters she constantly referred to a man called Paul Steinway; if I could find him we might succeed in tracing this man, but I have lost all hope of doing so." "That was the chap who was burnt in a lodging house in Pimlico," the detective said. "It's a pity that that fire happened, as we might have been able to make good use of him." Then as he glanced round the room his eye caught sight of a magazine on the table, lying open, with two photographs fac- ing each other. One was that of a beautiful girl with a sad face, and the other a man whom he recognised as Clifford Hawke. "Successful chap, that !" he said care- lessly. Jaspar looked at the photograph and flushed.

"Yes," he said curtly; "I suppose he is." "Do you know him well'?" the detective went on, glancing again at Clifford Hawke's cold, handsome face.

"I don't, and am not particularly anxious to continue the acquaintance," was the un- gracious reply. The detective smiled. lie was beginning to know the reason of his, Tennant's, changed appearance. Clifford Hawke had crossed him in love.

"I have never heard much about him," he said carelessly. "Is there anything you don't care particularly about in him?" "I dislike everything about him," Jaspar said savagely, "from his heady black eyes to the scar .on his wrist."

The detective turned sharply. "The scar on his wrist?" he repeated excitedly; "what kind of a scarf" -

BEAUTIEYING LONDON.

The erection of the imposing quadrigon the well-known arch at the top of COnstitu- tion Hill, shown in our picture, is now almost complete. The work is emblematic of the victory of Peace over War. It consists of four horses attached to a chariot, in which is a

figure of Peace. The mighty statue is by far the largest and heaviest yet produced in this country. The un-eiling by the King will take place shortly.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Dead Suffrage Bill.—The National League for Opposit g Women's Suffrage is naturally jubilant over the defeat of the Conciliation Bill, the first time a Woman Suffrage Bill has been rejected on second read- ing in t' e House of Commons, since the year 1881. In each of the past four years a Woman /Suffrage Bill has passed this stage by substantial majorities, and in some quartsrs there is a disposition to regard this change in the attitu-e of the House of Commons as a sure sign that n " Votes for Women " 11111 will be passed in this Parliament. Those who think otheraise derive some comfort from an analysis of the division on the Conciliation Bill, which mainly owed its defeat (by a runji rity of 14) to the action of the Irish Nationalists. Last year 31 Naticnalists voted for the Bill, this year 34 voted against, the turnover being else to a desire to keep the way clear for Home Rule. But for this opposition, the Bill would have rased its cond reading, despite the defection of some former surporters, owing to the public feeling aroused by the window smashing raids of the militants. Some of the more active spirits are DOW turning their thcughts to the power of the bo3cott, but there is also a movement on foot to try and get the militants to ahandon the policy of disorder, and unite all sections under the leadership of Mrs Henry Fawcett. That cause might afford some chance of securing a women's amendment to the Government's premised Reform Bill, but in any else it is certain that no progress will be made until the tactics involving injury to persons or property are abandon( d. Model Bachelor Quarters.—The confirmed bachelor continues to afford much innocent occupation for economists and moralists. How to cure him of his selfish 133 barit ism is an insistent problem, but the Simple Life Exhibition, whicb has just been held in London, will hardly help in finding a solution. It was net intended as an enterprise to promote colibecy, but it shows how easy it is to be happy, though single, both for bachelor-girls and men. In the model bachelor quarters one apartment can be used as a bedroom, sitting-room, kitchen, scullery and bathroom. It is all done by means of bed and bath cupboards, and a stove which can le a grate or a cooking raoge, and all the time serve as bath boiler. All these comforts with very little labour r trouble, and there are devices for- keeping food warm for many hours. Almost invariably, the girl who lives by herself contracts the habit of neglecting the "inner-woman," but with these up-to- date appliances, it is possible to return home at night to a hot meal which was prepared in the morning. Of course one need not be a bachelor man or worsen to enjoy such advantages. All they go to show is, that comfort in life is nut incompatible with cutting down house-keeping difficulties and expenses to a minimum. The Modish Nightcap.—To many, the very name of this particular accessary calls up visions ot a bag of calico, tied lightly round brushed back hair, but in no such spirit does the modern young woman translate the word. Originally the nightcap was intended to keep the wearer warm, but we cannot certainly associate warmth with the frilly web of net and costly lace, that now gees b 3, that name. The up-to-date nightcap is in high favour in the boudoir, during early morning hours, but it is to the invalid especially that it comes as a blessing end a boon, especially when she is just able to see her friends, and yet is quite unequal to the fatiguing business of hair dressing. Many women have a few little pin curls tacked across the front of their dainty nightcaps, so that in emergency all trouble of this description may be spared them.

Up-to-date DI illinery.—Hats are either very large and flat or very high and narrow, but the trimming in both styles is perched up as high as possible. Nearly every hat has an upturned brim, and this is liLed with the brightest of colours. OreGt sprays of many-hued flowers —stiffly mounted—are placed at the aide, a single bow of ribbon sufficing to softrn the rather bard effect. Unusual ;Wes of powder and pale blues are seen. All the new styles are in the home milliner.' favour, for flowers and feathers are always easy to manipulate, and these constitute almost the only trimming. It should be remembered that some faces require softness of out- line more than others. In thesc cases a frill of lace, or a fold of velvet is desirable. A fold is less hard than an edge of velvet.

Tics, Bows, and Jabots.—For those women who do not wear the low neck there are jabots in endless varieties, styles and shapes chiefly of net, lace,or finest lawn The side frill is decidedly waning in popularity. In ties and bows the difficulty lies in selecting from such a crowd of designs, each equally alluring. The ordinary wide-end necktie is always in fashion,and when arranged in a sailor knot is very effective and neat. The wide, straight tie is fastened in an artist's bow. Then there is the flat Qaaker bow,with or without ends, the swallow bow, spotted or plain, in silk or velvet. If these do not attract, the daintiest Lunches of flowers may be choeen. These are fastened on eafety pirs, and in some cases are made of satin touched with gold or oxidieed.Others are perfect imitations of flowers of the field and wayside, artistically arranged in prim. set order, or with hanging blooms that negligently break the monotony. No mere delightful finish fcr a plain blouse could be imagined. •

Toby Frills and Feather Boas.—No dissertation on r eckv. ear would be complete without a mention of the Toby frill which will in a few wrlits be worn by vast numbers of girls and wc men, and is especially recom- mended in fine tulle, mounted on a black velvet band and finished with a tiny flower or ornament in front. This frill is worn with :or without a collar and is suit- able for day or evening wear. Very welcome is the return of that old favourite, the curled ostrich feather boa. It has returned with a difference of course. It is now very long and very full, and its fronds are well curled. sometimes the fronds are arranged quite flat, and then cnly the tirs are curled. Shaded feather bons In beautiful effects are shown but this will probably prose but a passing creze. Concerning Sunshades.—Soon now our attention will naturally turn to sunshades, and it is are to predict that this year the Pagoda shape will lead the van. lhe plain silks with velvet borders and hanc'les to match ale charming, EC) also are the coloured tussores lined with spotted silk, in blue, cerise, and emerald green. The handles of these are white, or they harmonise with the linings. The elaborate sunshades for summer, and smart wear, are indescribably bright and elegant, but these are rot displayed to any great extent at the moment.

THE COUNCIL ELECTIONS.

The Urban District Council elections reached the climax on Saturday last when polling comn enced in each ward at 12 noon prompt. For the following eight hours the usual election scenes were witnessed ; enthusiastic cheering or booing as the respective candidates' carriages and motor cars, bearing voters and the familiar mottos went to and fro.

The presiding officers at the various polling booths were :—At Booth's Hill Ward, Mr T Newton ; Heatley Ward, Mr Denham ; and at Lymm Ward, Mr Oddie. Shortly after 8.20 p.m. the last ballot-box was delivered at the Council Chambers and the counting commenced. Watching the process, were the returning officer (Mr W Mullard), the candidates, their workers,members of the Council, and the election officers. The Lymm Ward votes were the first to be analysed, and the even piles of papers for each of the two candi- dates denoted a " close thing," end it was not until the actual figures were read that the tension was relaxed : Mr F E Evans, 80 ; Mr 11 11 Gotobed, 120. The Booths Hill ballot papers were the next to be counted, and it soon became evident that Mr Holt was " in," %%bid] surmise was proved correct when Mr. Mullard gave out the figures : Mr F W Dixon, 100 ; Mr J H Holt, 189. The Heatley Ward papers were the last to be exam'ned and again it was observed that the favours were pretty evenly distributed. The scrutiny complete, the Returning Officer announced the result as Mr P Shorrocke 147, Mr T 11 Warburton, 185. One of the pleasing features noticed was the hearty manner in which, in each case, the uasuccessful candi- date congratulated his successful opponent. Mr Holt proposed a vote of thanks to Mr Mullard for his able execution of the duties of Returning Officer ; end was seconded by Mr Evans. At ten minutes past nine Mr. Mullard led the way to the entrance of the Chambers where he proclaimed the results to the large crowd that bad gathered outside. After reading out the figures he declared John Hardy Holt duly elected for Booths Hill similarly he declared Thomas Henry Warburton, and Richard Harry Gotobed elected as representatives for Heatley and Lymm Wards respectively. Judging by the cheers which greeted the announcement of each result, the Lymm Ward issue seemed to be the popular victory. Mr Gotobed, amid cheers stepped to the front and In a brief speech said be thanked the electors of Lymm Ward from the bottom of his heart for the vote of confidence they had placed in him. He also thanked his workers and especially his chairman. He also expressed his gratitude to Mr Evans for the clean and straight manner in which their fight had been carried through and hoped that be (Mr Evans) would get his requital. Mr Warburton was the next to come forward and after expressing his gratitude for the victory that Heatley Ward bad given him, he said that they had been the best of friends during the fight end he hoped that they would continue to be so. "It will be my effort to serve you to the best of my ability." As the cheers that greeted Mr Holt were diminishing a voice was heard to cry out, " Give it a bit more mortar." " Yes," replied Mr Holt, " we've plastered it on to-day. We West Enders don't do things by halves, a two-to-one majority is our style." He then tendered his sincere thanks for the confidence that the Booth's Hill Ward had placed in him. Mr Evans said be first wished to thank the 80 voters and the workers who bad supported him. Ile next con- gratulated Mr Gotobed on his victory, and concluded his trim speech by saying that though they did not want him as a Councillor, if he could he would only be too pleased to serve them in any other way. Mr Shorrocks, in a pithy address, said : The apprentice is defeated hut not disgraced and certainly not down- hearted. I have done my best, and will, if possible, do even better in the future. lam proud that 148 persons should have recorded their votes in my favour." After congratulating Mr Warburton, he concluded with : "And now some of you can go home contented and others discontented." Mr Dixon thanked the 100 electors who had given him their votes, and said the result was a foregone conclusion,but he thought that theyhad done well for a preliminary canter, especially when he considered the late entry into the field and his small band of workers. He said he appreciated the manner in which the contest had been conducted. The following is the full list of results :

BOOTH'S HILL WARD. Mr J H Holt lB9 Mr T F W Dixon

Majority HRATLEV WARD.

Mr T II Warburton • lB5 Nr P Shorrocks

Majority

M M WAR D. Mr R H Gotobed l2O Mr F E Evans

Majority the reports in the daily papers tell of a tide of distre3s rapidly rising in the large towns, where the main centres of industry are situated. It is difficult to regard this most serious state of things otherwise than as a mark of Divine displeasure on the scientific selfishness that has been alloged to grow up among all classes of our population. The greater light that has been vouch- safed to us in to remarkable a degree during late years has been used almost entirely for the aggrandisement of men with very little thought of the glory of God ; the general standard of comfort has risen appreciably till it has developed into a refinement of luxury, and it is only through a crisis such as the present that a more rational view of life can be recovered.

At a meeting held in Liverpool' on Saturday, lir. George Newburn, of Ileathside School, was, without opposition, elected as the vice-president of the Lanca- shire Federation of Assistant Teachers' Association.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

MR. J. A. SEDDON ON TEIE WORKERS'

REVOLT. THE MESSAGE OF THE BULLET.

Mr J A Seddon, ex-M.P. for Newton, addressing a meeting of parochial electors in the National Schools, Haydock, on Saturday evening, recalled that Mr Asquith the other day said that was not a time for manifestoes but for straight talking That was the position of the Labour Party. The manifesto of the Labour Party did not change, for they always claimed that the worker was entitled to a greater share of the wealth that he produced, and until that share was obtained then the Labour Party was out to fight for the workers against all corners. Mr. Seddon ridiculed Lord Wolmer's assertion that Tariff "Reform " was the only solution for the Labour troubles of the country. Tariff " Reform" had tot solved the Labour troubles of France, Germany, and the United States. Mr Seddon upheld the miners in their grand struggle for a minimum wage, and said that the solidarity of their racks and action was the admiration of the nations of the Con- tinent. The strike was forcing into the arena of practical politics the questicn of the nationalisation of the mines. The Tory remedy to bring them back to their work was the armed forces of the Crown. Mr F E Smith mei others had said that the selfish and blackmailing miners must be brought to a reasonable frame of mind and must not be allowed to hold up the resources of the country, and, if need be, the armed forces must be brought in to protect the willing worker. If a man deserted his fellows and ran away on the field of battle he was shot as a coward, but if he was willing to sell his fellow-labourers and become a blackleg he was a hero. But there was just as much heroism in standing by their fellow-workmen in an industrial battle as there was in standing against the bullets on the field of battle, and there was as much cowardice in deserting their comrades of industry as there would be in a soldier running away on the battlefield. Yes, the armed forces of the Crown were coming,the soldiers were now moving. God forbid that they should shoot, but if they did those who had directed them, and not least the Tory party, would hear in every bullet that sang its message of death the strong note of revolt so far as the workers of this country were concerned. (Applause) Never in the history of industrial democracy was there a grimmer or firmer determination to see that industrial struggles should be settled, not by bullets, but by the granting of a minimum wage—the just and reasonable demand of the miners. He believed in peaceful settle- ment of their disputes, but if they were going to use the armed forces of the Crown then he was certain that on the heels of revolt would follow revolution. They must make no mistake, not only the miners but the great body of workers in every industry were incensed by the fact that the miners had been flouted by the British House of Commons. They had been told by their statesmen of all parties that they were not worth ss. a day, that it would not be wise or statesmanlike to put into an Act of Parliament the bed- rock principle that the colliers of Great Britain were worth 5s a day. If the miners were not worth 5s a day then the coal was not worth getting. If colossal fortunes were being made out of the trade—and he was told that the collieries in the immediate neighbourhood had made a profit of over £30,000 during the last few days out of what had for a long time been considered little better than rubbish—if the mining industry could pay an average of 91 per cent., as it chd last year, then it could pay, and it must pay, and it must pay before the miners went back to work. Industrial democracy was beginning to recognise its power, and was standing together as it never had done in the history of this or of any other country. RELIEVING DISTRESS AT EARLESrOWN. INSURANCE MEN'S SECOND EFFORT.

Mr Jos Fairhurst and his staff of agents are still pursuing their efforts on behalf of those affected with the strike. On Saturday evening they gave a hot-pot supper to about 200 women, and afterwards a free concert was held. The meeting was held in the assembly rooms, Hotel-street, kindly placed at the dis- posal of the workers by the Earlestown Progressive Spiritualist Society. This is the second effort the insurance men have put forward on behalf of the dis- tressed of the town, and they were well repaid for their labours, by the appreciative manner in which the hungry women partook of so substantial a repast. At 5 o'clock they sat down to a good meal made of hot- pot, bread, and new milk to drink. After supper the room was cleared for the concert, which commenced at 7 o'clock. In the absence of Mr Baron, of St. Helens, Mr Fair- hurst presided, and was supported on the platform by Mr J W Marsden and Mr 0 A Leach of the Warrington District. In the course of his remarks, the Chairman stated their reasors for having taken up the work of helping the distressed. Continuing he said that a good many people had been curious to know how they had been able to so successfully carry out the work already done. Some people had unkindly criticised them, but that did not count, they had gone on with their work, believing that it was necessary. (Applause.) He had gone into some of the homes and had found parents and children almost without even a bite of bread. Such conditions were enough to satify him that something ought to be done, and done immediately. (Applause.) Mr Fairhurst then stated that all those who would take part in the concert programme, had given of their services free of charge. (Applause). The following artists very ably rendered their respective items :—Miss Stronach and Miss Simms, a duet "In the Springtime"; Mr J Rigby, Lancashire reading "Soup for a sick mom"; Mr R King gave two items, recitation "The last act" and a humorous monologue entitled "Oh, memory " ; Mr Daniel Stet, es gave comic sketches for which he was frequently encored, causing roars of laughter; Miss Simms gave "Oh prom'se me " ; Mr S Lyth gave an able selection on the Dulcimer; Mr Rose, a recitation "The fireman's wedding." The accompanists were Miss A Booth and Mr T Preston. Mr Leach in proposing a vote of thanks to all those who had helped to make the work so successful, said he did not want those who had been assisted in any way to look upon it as a matter of charity. (Hear, hear). It was a question of duty as fellow citizens to help one another in times of distress. It meant simply, that some of them were without means and those who were more fortunately placed, bad given, end given most freely, and with a generous heart. (Applause). Con- tinuing, the speaker said, what surprised him most was the woederful patience, and the tolerance with which the hungry bore their unfortunate lot. In times like these it was indeed a difficult matter to keep one's head. But no real good could come by force, The best and only way to real progress was by adopting commonsense methods. Just recently one of the great leaders of labour had been criticised for making a certain statement, but he (the speaker) believed that there lay behind those utterances an earnest desire to work out the industrial problems with- out the shedding of blood. (Hear, hear). All honour to snob men. We ought to be gad that we were led by men who faced these difficulties with cool, calm, common-sense judgment. Mr. Leach then enumerated the kind gilts they had received. Some people who were complete strangers to those present had sent of their best. One gentleman from Cheshire had sent them about 60 gallons of new milk, and this enabled them to continue further assistance during the coming week. (Applause). Then there was the noble army of workers and first of all Mr Fairhurst who together with his staff bad so well organised the scheme. Mrs Fairhurst end Mrs Marsden had had some splendid assistance given to them by lady friends. The tradespeople of Earlestown had been most generous in their gifts of bread, meat and vegetables. The co-operative society had rendered them invaluable assistance in many ways. Mr Horner had given milk, and they were indebted to the Progressive Spiritualist Society for the free use of the rooms, light and piano. Everyone had worked hard for the real love of helping others, and they would be best thanked by the knowledge of a speedy settlement to all these troublesome times. (Loud applause). Mr Marsden moved a vote of thanks to the artists. Mr J Smith seconded and the vote wes passed. At the close, the chairman announced that 120 pints of new milk would be given on the following Sunday morning to the most needy cases.

HOW NOT TO LIVE. RUNCORN FAMILIES' FATAL BELIEF.

"There is a disposition among certain families to pig together, regardless of every decent sanitary precautron with chimneys packed, doors and windows closed, and every conceivable method adopted to exclude pure air, in the fatal belief that the room is being kept warm. Runcorn's medical officer of health (Dr li.l.`Dougsll) made these observations in his report for 1911, which was received by the Urbsn Council on Wednesday evening. The chief danger to the town's bill of health he stated was overcrowding. There were 2EO deaths registered during 1911 ; 90 were infants under the age of one year, and of these 32 died from diarrhcea. in August. Only 492 births were registered, 23 fewer than in 1910 a birth-rate of 28 1 per 1,000, which, the doctor remarked, was exceedingly low and constituted a grave outlook.

A GOOD CASE.

The appreciation of the public,, the com- mendation of doctors, and the support of the Press make together a very good case in favour of margarine as a food. Those who have never used margarine before would do well to cast all prejudice aside and just try 'Stork,' by far the finest Margarine obtainable. They ‘vill discover, no doubt to their surprise, that they can detect no difference in flavour or appearance between • Stork ' Margarine and Choice country butter. In the certificate of merit awarded for 'Stork' Margarine by the Incorporated Institute of Hygiene, there is sufficient evidence of quality; medical testi- mony proves a standard of food value as high as that of butter; and the economy is obvious—indeed, it means a saving of half.

STATE INSURANCE.

THE ORDER OF RECHABITES AND

THE ACT.

In view of the fact that the Insurance Act is to come into operation in July the various friendly societies in the country are making preparations to work it to the best advantage. They are calling together all their members for the purpose of explain- ing to them the position of their organisations under' the measure and endeavouring to increase their mem- bership. With this object the Warrington distlict of of the Independent Order of Rechabites held a teaparty followed by an enthusiastic meeting in the Friars Green school on Saturday. About 120 of the officers and workers from the various tents of the d strict sat down to an excellent tea, the catering for which was in the able hands of Sisters Fernley, Palin, Fellows, War burton, Shaw, Powell, Hamblett and Wright. The subsequent Conference was presided over by Bro. JAMES SHAW, who after a brief introduction explaining the purpose of the Conference which was to give the officers of the Tent as much information as possible, to enable them to administer the Act when it came into operation, called upon Bro. Richardson Campbell, High Secretary of the Order, to give an address.

---- - Bro. CAITITELL prefaced his address by staling that if we wanted to find the reason for the introduction of the Insurance Act, we must go back to the last report of the Royal Commission on the Poor Law system which stated that there were some millions of workers in this country who made no provision either against sickness or unemployment, and when times of sickness and distress came upon them they had to seek Poor Law relief, yet he was ruepared to prove that the working classes as a whole were not altogether unthrifty, and gave statistics showing from different sources the accumulation of millions of pounds, due to the thrift of the working classes of the country. Be then gave a very able exposition of the Insurance Act and how it would affect the Friendly Sccieties, and in particular the Order of Rechabites. A large number of questions were asked by the workers present, and satisfactorily answered by the speaker. At the close a very hearty vote of thanks was accorded to Bro. Campbell for the splendid explana- tion he had given of the Act. After the Conference a public meeting was held, at which Mr. J. Jarman presided. Mr. JARMAN, in opening the proceedings, said that so far as he was concerned, he was of the opinion that the National Insurance Act was going to be one of the finest pieces of legislation the country had ever had. (Hear, hear.) So far as they, as members of a friendly society, were concerned, it was not going to do them any harm, but would improve their position very materially. There were some things for which they in that society had fought for a long time and at this stage of the proceedings they would have to fight harder. They as a society had long been engaged in a fight for raising the children, which was outside the woik of other friendly societies. (Hear, bear). There were few other friendly societies with junior lodges but now the Bechabites, had an even better opportunity of getting hold of the juniors. It was an unfortunate thing that some of the other friendly societies had no other meeting place than the public house which was no place for young people to go to under any circumstances. He felt that theirs being a temperance organisation, they would be able to offer as large or even large benefits than any of the existing friendly societys. They all realized that so far as total abstainers were concerned, they had far less serious sickness than any of those who were in the habit of taking strong drink so under the Act, they would probably be able to give increased benefits. In conclusion he hoped they would get hold oi many of the young children and that would save them from getting into loose habits and help them to avoid the use and abwe of strong drink. (Applause). Bro. J. H. LEAR CATON, member of the Board of Management at the outset of his address said they were not there to criticise the Act but now that it was law they should see how it affected them. He believed there was no measure, not only in living history but in legislative history of over 100 years that had affected so many people at one and the same time as the great National Insurance Act. It was a measure of absolute first rate importance. There were some people who had objected to anything in the nature of compulsory insurance and who would have been delighted if the old system had prevailed. Continuing the speaker said that when they considered that in the last decade they bad doubled their membership, the Reohabites had no complaint to make about the voluntary system. But the point was that they had not succeeded in getting all the people in the friendly societies, so that the scheme had to he made compulsory I He believed the day fixed for the Act coming into operation was the 15th July, and that day was St. Svvithen's day and the Act was going to give untold blessings to the British people. The speaker then eloquently explained the payments and benefits in the Act, and after snaking a brief reference to the coal strike and expressing the hope that everything would be settled at an early date he said that one benefit of the Act over the Friendly Society was that a man could not fall into arrears when he was sick but only when he was out of employ- ment. The scheme was not a plutocratic scheme like the German chancellors scheme. If it bad been a non- contributory meaasure raised by taxation it would have been a plutocratic scheme. If it had been a non- contributory scheme all the teetotallers and abstainers would have been locked together with all the other people of the nation. Ho would have called that an injustice to the abstaining community of the country. (Hear, hear.) They as abstainers had got to pay through rates and taxes for mopping up the slops of the liquor traffic. It was high time they revolted. The traffic existed to satisfy the people who liked it, and they should bear the burden and not the abstainers. (Hear, hear.) Through the Act the people could pay their insurance through any approved ar ciety, and they as rechabites could choose their own tent. (Hear, hear.) Although the Act had come in they were not going to lower the temperance flag. It was a well known fact that abstainers lived longer than indulgers. The abstainers reaped in length of days the results of their abstinence, and their rates of sickness was far less than any other s. If they were going to make a healthier nation they could only do it by restricting the drinking habits and customs of the people. The maternity I enefits if properly administered would con- tribute largely to the bringing up of an efficient race of citizens. No money granted in that way would be wasted on drink if they had their own Order as the approved society. (Hear, hear). Their society had done a great deal in the past for the uplifting of the nation and the common weal, and they were determined to keep on that good work onder the Insurance Act. (Applause). A pleasing musical programme was submitted, songs being very creditably rendered by Miss Elsie Hewitt and Mr. G. H. Gough. Mr. David Sutton caused much amusement with his humorous items. Miss Jarman was an able accompanist. Bro C S FELLows proposed a vote of thanks to the artistes, and also to Mr Caton for his able address. Bro WRIGIIT seconded and the proposition was heartily carried. Mr JARMAN and Mr CATON suitably responded.

HEARTS OF OAK SOCIETY AND THE ACT.

Probably no friendly society has been meniioned and quoted more in regard to the Insurance Act than the Hearts of Oak Benefit Society, which has been able to strange a most attractive scale of benefits for its members. The officials of the local branch must have been well satisfied with the large number of persons who assembled in the Wesleyan schoolroom, Bold- street, on Wednesday evening, when several prominent officials of the Society dealt at some length and in a lucid manner wish the provisions of the National Insur- ance Act, and its effect upon members of friendly 'societies. Bro. ALBERT BINKS presided, and be was supported by some of the officials of the local branch and many other gentlemen connected with the society. In open- ing the proceedings the Chairman said that was a unique occasion in regard to their friendly society, inasmuch as it was the first time they bad had the pleasure of entertaining their worthy president (Mr. R. Green.) (Hear, hear.) He felt sure they would all appreciate ' his presence that night and were all looking forward to hearing what his views were on the great Insurance Act. (Hoar, hear.) 'A hatever the conditions of the Act might be they all knew that the basis of their society was on the soundest financial lines. (Hear, hear.) Every member ought to take a deep interest in the advance- ment of his society. As they were all aware, many of their members were engaged in trades in which acci- dents were constatt. They would find in connection with the Act and on reading the reports of the society, that this matter was dealt with for the old members, who had been privileged to lay down the foundation on which the society had been built. The society com- manded a reserve fund of £4,000,000 and that showed they were en a safe footing. (Hear, hear.) They naturally wanted to know what the young people were going to receive when they became members. Ile felt that wss a question which demanded sone answer. At present if the members came under compensation tb ey were assured by the society that they glt their com- pensation plus their sick pay. The Act, however slid that a member could not take his sick pay along with the compensation. Those were matters they wanted clearing up. (Applause). Bro lt GREEN (Sherringham) president of the society said that friendly societies had started when people found themselves so oppressed by circumstances that they found it impossible to put any money away for sickness or disablement. Therefore the grand principles of friendly societies came to light and provided for those people something which would insure their independence of the Poor Law. Through friendly societies the reolle in these

days of adversity brought on by sickness and disablement had been able to look with contempr on the arm of the poor law and walk on their own dignity. (Hear, hear.) They had a membership of over 300,000 scattered about all over the world. (Applause.) Since the inception of the society they had paid £7,930,513 12s. 9d. in sick pay. When they tried to grasp the enormity of those [figures they found it nearly impossible. Continuing Mr. Green said thatithrough the Insurance Act all citizens vvouldlbecome interested In friendly societies because they would ,bave to contribute something towards the support of the principles of friendly society ism. After having explained the payments and benefits derivable under the Act, the speaker said they were one of the few friendly societii s who had paid already 20s for maternity benefits. He believed the Hearts of Oak society was the first society to put its foot down on the principle of payment "in kind" with the result that Lloyd George yielded and the benefits would be paid in cash. He thought the greatest benefit that could be conferred on the toiling working class popula- tion was the great sanatoria benefit. That benefit alone would be well worth the fourpance per week they were called upon to contribute. The advantage of having free medical attendance and free medicine was one which they had not enjoyed in the past. He had heard nothing of

T. Hanewsy, who appeared on behalf of the Corporation, said that the Inspector of Scales (Mr. Wallington) visited the shop on the 18th March and found a pair of scales, the verifi- cation stamp of which had been defaced. [he serious part of the case was that when the scales had been rejected the defendant still used them. Defendant said he was under the impression that he could use the scales until October next. They bad always put the scales right at the office when they had been examined. Mr Wallington pointed out that if they had been repaired he could have used them until that date. YOUNG COUPLE'S QUARREL.—CharIes Budworth, 25, West-street, was summoned by his wife Alice of 54, Plumpton-street, for assault. Both parties are young. Complainant stated that on the 25th March she was waiting for a friend at the corner of Rylands-street, when a friend of her husband's asked her to go for a drink or a walk. She had been separated from her husband since Christmas and he had repeatedly sent men to her to ask similar questions. Her husband got hold of her umbrella and after hitting her with it he broke it and threw it awet.—Defendant denied having hit his wife and evidence in support of this statement was given by the friend who also denied having asked the complainant to go for a drink or a walk.—Evidence was given for the complainant to the effect that defendant did hit her with the umbrella and this witness also stated that the defendant carried a bottle of poison in his pocket and had threatened to finish his wife. Defendant denied this statement and said the stuff was Condy's fluid. He was ordered to pay 10s.

Burning -Fatality. Mr S Brighouse conducted an inquest at the War- ! ingtcn Police Station, on Wednesday, into tLe circumstances of the death of Ellen Braide, the six- year-old daughter of George and Cathetine Braide, 293, Winwick-road, who died in the Infirmary on Sunday from severe burns. The mother stated that on Saturday morning the deceased was playing in the street, when she complained of feeling cold. She was told to go and warm herself by the fire. The fire- guard and fender had been taken away preparatory to the cleaning of the fireside. Whilst standing against the fire the wind blew the flames out of the grate and set the child's pinafore on fire, and she sustained severe burns about the body. A verdict of " Accidental death " was returned.

Smith, M.P., and the Wire Purchase Problem.

Sir,—Now that Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., appears to be taking an interest in local affairs, perhaps he will not deprive himself of the pleasure of a little political combat with a gentleman who puts forward a proposi- tion from a purely detached point of view. I hope he will not raise the excuse that I am not epeaking for Mr. Crosfield ar.d therefore he cannot take notice of my letter. I have written him before under the above heading but he was too busy then to reply. I put it to him, however, that be has stated on more than one occasion that he would at all times devote his attention and energy to the interests of the gre3t constituency which he was so proud to represent. This is a matter of considerable importance to Warrington and I venture to hope that he will not forget his promise. It is reported in the Preas this morning that the Birkenhead Town Council has eanctioned the plach g of a contract for the supply of electric cables for the year with the London agency of a German firm. It appears that their contract was over IS per cent. lower than the next English tender in one case, and 19 per cent. lower in another. We are also told that there is a Conservative majority ill the Town Council, and that the motion was can ied without remark.

Now, Sir, what I would like Mr. Harold Smith to tell us is whether he believes it to be economically "sound " for the Birkenhead Town Council to "worship the god of cheapness above all others" by purchasing electric cables frcm a German film, and economically "unsound " for the Warrington Cor- poration to buy wire from Germany on similar terms. —Thanking you, yours truly,

-EDMUND OSBORNE.

===== 0002601_19120406_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
22, Bold-street, Warrington, 4th April, 1912.

Charity or Work ?

Sir,— Apropos of the p esent distress exlsting amongst the working class d Warrirgton, caused by the stoppage of the various woks ovcing to this disastrous coal dispute. May I, as a working man, review the means taken to alleviate this distress ?

In the first instance may I tender my thanks, and I think in so doing I echo the opinion of every working man, to the directors of the various works in so promptly coming to the aid of their employees by opening soup kitchens for their benefit, also to Sir Gilbert Greenall, Messrs. Crosfield and Mr. H. Smith, M.P., and all others who have so generously responded to the appeal for help in rur present trouble. I say all praise be given to them for their generous intentions. But are their intentions being carried out in the way the Mayor's fund is being distributed ? May I give you an instance? A man who is married but has no children receives from the place where he works two pints of soup and one and a half pounds of bread daily, cut of a fund provided by the directors of the firm and contributed to by his fellow workmen who are fortunate enough to be employed ain:ting the crisis - a fund absolutely independent of the Mayor's fund. This man whilst walking past the offices of the education department in Sankey-street, sees a notice pasted on the door posts : 'That men employed at the various works iu Warrington are invited to walk in." He walked in and received a form to fill up. He returned the same to the office and was told that he was not eligible for relief as he and his wife were in receipt of soup and bread from the place where he (the husband) worked So evidently the maycr's fund is not for the benefit of the man who is thrown out of work owing to the coal strike. He must depend on his employers and his fellow workmen for relief. I hardly think this was the intention of the donors to the fund. Ido not think they would have raised any seriouB objection to the uteaten in the case receiving, say, a little tea and sugir ? But sir, there is another aspect to the case. That there is distress in the town is evident when we con- sider that over :5,000 wage earners are coming home on Saturdays n ith empty pockets, instead of baying from ic.:l upwards to hand over to their wives. So we cannot shat our eyes to the fact that where there are no wages there is no food and none of the comforts that tend to make a workingman's home a happy ore. Now sir, as these facts are well-known to "the man in the street" is it not the duty of " the powers that be" in our Municipal Council to at once open relief works—for the unemployed—instead of depending on prirate charity. There is plenty of scope for the employment of the unemployed in street and road improvements in Warrington, and if that is not feasible, why not give married men a chance of doing relief work in the Workhouse and receiving remuneration in kind or cash. 13Llieve me, sir, when I say that there is nothing more abhorre it to the true working man than to be the recipient of charity, either public or private. It takes away what every English- man worthy of the name is proud of—his independ- ence. The workingman of Warrington, whilst deeply grateful to all who have helped him in his hour of need, asks for woriz, so that lie may preserve his character- istics as a workingman and an Englishman, and it lies with the Corporation to give him that work.

A WORKINGMAN.

HOCKEY.

TIIB LATCHFORD CLUB.

The First Eleven finished the season with a win of 2-1 over Wallasey. The play was mostly in Latchford's favour but the forwards were not in their best shooting form. In addition, Ridley in the Wallasey goal pla3ed a great game, showing good judgment in running oat and dispossessing the opposing attacks before clear shots could be got at him. Travis shot both goals for Latchford. The defence on both sides was pretty sound. The goal obtained by Wallasey was very peculiar' the ball swerving unexpectedly into the net through the high wind. The Seconds met at Wallasey and Latchford won 5-2. Here again the visiting forwards missed several good chances in front of goal or Wallasey would have been beaten heavily. The visitors defence was all right, the half backs in particular playing well, Bradshaw at centre being the pick. The goals were shot by Banks (2), Colton, Minards and Starkey, one each. Banks played a nice game at centre forward and kept the line togethr l side

_ The match was not without its comical aii. for a sudden gust, of wind in the first half blew both goals down at the same time; they were soon erected again. The complete results for the past, mason are : Goals

_ Played Won Lost Drawn For Agst First Eleven 25 14 5 6 101 72 Second Eleven 2O 12 5 3 81 30 The record, taken all round, is a good one, because better class clubs have been met this last season and the games have been closer. The First Eleven for the second season in succession have scored over 100 goals which speaks well for the forwards. The defence in the early part of the season had to undergo changes but latterly this Las been avoided and less goals have been registered by opponents as a result. The Second Eleven,owing to First Team calls, has had to be changed rather frequently, but their record is veld, if not so brilliant as the previous year. An even more improved fixture list hasalready been arranged for nest season and everything augurs well for next September. The animal general meeting will be held at Atkinson's Cafe, Bridge-street, on Friday evening, April nth, at 8 p.m.

USELESS SUFFERING.

Although the world is full of pain and misery which nothing can avert, it is now known that fully one-half the suffering of womankind is utterly useless and one could be prevented if only a few facts about it eould be made better known. The Examiner, Saturday, April, 6, 1912.

LADY GREENALL OUT WITH THE BELVOIR.

The whole question of fuel economy is closely allied t--) that of smoke prevention whether in the boiler, the furnace, or the domestic fire grate, wherever there is smoke there is waste. Boot( r Shaw estimates that 70 rer cent, of the London smoke is caused by domestic fires, leaving 30 per ceat, due to chimneys of manufactories. But if statistics could be taken of a town like Warrir gfcn the figurfis would probably be reversed. W hen bituinincus coal is fed on the top of a burning fire you have a condiiien similar to that in whioh gas is distilled from coal. But when coal is distilad the gases are collected while in the open Ilie ycu have the gas, tarry produces, and smoke, carried up the chirnney,and the solid portions as they are cooled in the air falhand distiibute themselves all around. In the fire grate arte,r coals are put on the heat is below and the coal above, which as it heats giva s off unburet tar vap ,ur and coal gas. This smoke largely consists of very minute vesicles which are constantly in motion. When these get into the air they float about until they come int) contact with a slid sur- face, which contact bursts the little liquid envelope, filming a m;croseopic drop of tar and the contaked gases are liberated. Thus all the foliage of our town shrubs get coated not so much with soot as with a greasy tarry sul stance. There is no doubt that putting coals on the top of the fire is wrorg. They ought to be put in underneath the red-hot pait of the fire, but the practical difficulty of doing this has not so far been surmounted. When the fe e flames have burst through and ignited the escaping vapours we have the luminating flame on the surface of the fire, and the was.e which was going en rr duced. But even at this p,riod you have not complete combustion. It , wil be noticed that the flams at their pointed crest constantly give off a wavy column of smoke. This smoke is mainly soot in the form of minute panicles of carbon. As Ltirne passes the fire burns clear, but even although the gases are not visible a considerable quantity of carbon oxidels given off Professor V. B. Lesis gives it as his opinion that the only way in which the amok) proble n can be ' solved is by stopping the direct combustion of bituminous coals bi grates, and employing the gas works or the country to convert it int ) gaseous and s Aid fuel, both of which would give us smokeless combusti n, at the same time extracing in useful form those volatile products which, in the manufacture of gag, yield tar, but in the seo7e or grate form smoke or a tarry chara, ter. What he means is that our coal be e first treafoi to rid it of its easily volatilized products, but th it the pr cess should net be carried so far as to convert it into coke as we know it

One patent file grate I have lately seen has a chamber for th? coal at the back of the fire. The coals are fed in horn thi top and as the fire burns work their icay down the chamber to the base of the fire. With the surrounding heat the v.,latile mttr in the ex.] is to some extent released, and escapes first downiven.ls and then through the red hot fire where it becomes burnt. By the time the coals get to the actual fire they are partiAlly coked. This appears to be working on the right principle, but I do not know what success in practice this form of grate has achieved.

Pethaps the most startling suggestion which has been put forward during the recent crisis t 3 effect economy in use and labour is that of the scientist Sir William Ramsey. He proposes t 3 do away altogsther with the bringing of coal to the surface and suggests that it should be burned in the pit under a regulated supply of air and brought to the surface by pipes in the form of power gas with electric generating stations on the pit bank and the electric piwer distri- buted by cable. It appears simple on paper but as a novice one wonders how the powers below. are to behave as they ought without human supervision, and if this is essential how this supervision is possible in an atmosphere of power gas. Still more unlikely things have been accomplished by the help of science, and the future can alone determine whether in this suggestion we have the germ of ideas which will revolutionize one of our largest and our most dangerous industries.

COALS.

BY C. E. PARKER.

liking is a very ancient industry. There is a vezy fine passage in that noble poem the book of Job in which the loneliness of the miner's 1 fe is portrayed in striking imagery. 'the m;rer uudtrground in the darkness and blackness of death, forgotten by those who walk above, turniug up the mountains from their base, bringing bread out of the uuderparts of the earth ; are vivid touches. The f Alowiug translation is that of SArnuel COX written before the last revision of the Bible, and is strikingly confirmeu by the new vers-on.

" Surely there is a vein for silver, And a place for the gold which men wash out; Iron may be taken from the earth, And the rock be smelted for copper. (The Miner) maketh an end of darkness, And searcheth through all its limits For the stonr- sof darkness ant of t,e blackness of death; He sioketh a shaft far from the habitations of men, He is forgotten of those who walk above, Fie swing snspeaded afar from men.

The undi:rparts Of the earth, out of which cometh fotb bread, Ar 3 stirred up as if by fie; The rocks are the sapphire's bed. And yield him gold-dust. -.. . _

That. Path ! no gird of prey knoweth it. Nor bath the eye of tl.e ha%lr scanned it ; No pr,uily-pacing beast hath trodden it, Nor lion passed by upon it. He putteth forth his ta,:d against the gnartß, He turneth up the mountains from their base ; He cutteth out canals among the rocks, And his eye detecteth every precious thing; He bindeth up waters so that they weep not, And bringeth that which is bidden to light." The above does not mention co 1. This has only been mined to any extent in mo !ern times. It is evident however that the Romans knew of coal and used it, for in several of the Roman encampments in this country coal cinders have been found. We read in the times of King Edward I, that 1,200 cart-loads of pit roal, or fossil fuel as they then called it, were lecoived by the Abbey of Peterbonugh. "By pro- clamation King Edward I. prohibited the burning of sea coal in London and the suburbs, to avoid the sulferous smoke and savour of the firing," and in the same proclamation commanded all persons to make their tires of wood 1300.

It is a strange transition for the weirdness of this old world poem and early English records to the pr saic realites which have faced the nation in con- nection with the present coal strike. The " under- parts of the earth" have had a rest in this country for four weeks. Industry is like some monster mot-:r driven by a thormnd cylinders which during the last Liv weeks have one by one gne out of action. The speed has day by day, and hour by 11, ur, become slower, and a funeral stillness crept over the giant manufacturies of the country:, the great industrial machine has lost it hum and its rhythm. Referring to the dangers that surround the life of the miner, the following are the official figures. The toll which miners pay in death and accident is a very heavy one, and appears to be increasing. On the average four men are killed every day in the year, white five hundred are disabled every day by accident which prevents their working for seven days or over. Minor accidents are therefore not included..

-.,. ....-....,-............. - Number Number injured Injured as and disabled for Number reported to more than seven Year. Good cometh out of evil, and in this as in all thingsl the good may be more permanent than the evil. OLe result of the recent trying time is the revelation bow coal can be economised. It has been a surprise to ' not a few how it is possible when necessity demands to cut down consumption. We have heard of public institutions in which the coal used has been got down to below one-half the normal without doing away with any fires, simply by those in control giving their thought to ecomical use of the fuel. Private houses with a supply that under normal coalitions would last about ten daps, have been able to make their stock last until the end of the strike. This has been done without any change in the fire grate, but by simple !methods which have reduced the speed of consump- tion, pokers have had a rest. cinders have been com- pletely burnt, the draught has heed recontrolled, and the hours when a fire was required reduced. But a great deal depends upon the style of the fire grate, and the last word has not by any means been said in regard to this. Great advances have been made in reducing coal consumed by the introduction of improved forms of grate, but much remains to be done. I was told of one who during the strike adopted a simple method of damping down a fire to save coals. In his home a fire was only wanted in the morning and late afternoon, after the fire had done its work in the morning and gone quiet be placed over it a cover of wet brown paper, and on the top of this put wet small coal. This fire kept smouldering until it was required about half past five when by the help of a poker it was brought into active service. Such a plan would only be useful when, as in this case, the fire was not required during the day. It would certainly not do to boil potatoes.

Perhaps the chief reason why progress is so slow is that the houses we live in and the grates we use are not as a rule our ewn. The wasteful grate is the landlord's the loss falls on the tenant. At best we can only get for varining a room by a fire grate radiated heat, the hot gases and smoke are carried up the chimney and their heat is lost. From the point of economy the open fire -grate is extremely wasteful. At the same time it is healthy, being probably the best form of ventilator that can be imagined, constantly changing the air. Its cheery glow is always welcome and the failure of many new forms of domestic heat production is due not that they fail in economy, but because they do not give the homely feeling the presence of which makes us tolerate the extravagance Examples of the old fashioned wasteful grate can be
