SEQUEL TO A LOAN. -- - COUNTY COURT ACTION AS TO OWNERSHIP OF FURNITURE.

An inter-pleader action remitted from the High Court was heard at the Warrington County Court, on Thurs- day by his Honour Judge Stanger, KC. The parties were Mrs hey Green, widow of the late Mr Thomas Green, of Knutsford-road, the execution creditor ; Mrs Alice Rigby, 40, Church-street, the defendant ; and Mr Thomas Mason, 40, Church-street, the claimant. The issue to be tried was whether the sum of .£lO5 which hsd been paid into Court, representing the value of furniture, ecc., belonged to the execution creditor or to the claimant. Mr Acton (instructed by Messrs Arthur Browne and Co.), appeared for the claimant Mr A T Miller (instructed by hlessrs Forsha l.eorshaw) for the execution creditor.

and land

Mr. Acton explained that the judgment debtor was the wife of Mr. Peter Rigby, who carried on business as a builder until 1905, when he became bankrupt. The claimant was a brother of Mrs Rigby, and had been living with the Rigby's for several years. At the bank- ruptcy proceedings Mrs Rigby and Mr Mason claimed and proved their title to all the household furniturs and effects at 90, Church-street. The action in respect of which execu•lon was issued was brought against Mrs Rigby for a loan of £lOO, and a writ was issued with the remit that £1.05 was paid into Court in respect of the furniture which had b,en seized by the Sheriff, After Mr Rigby's bankruptcy there was no money going into the house, and from titre to time Mr Mason advanced sums for the support of his sister. As security a portion of the furniture was conveyed to him under a deed prepared by Messrs Arthur Browne and Co. In February 1910 the rest of the furniture was transferred to Mr Mason who bad continued to make advanoes for the support of his sister. The claim, Mr Acton further explained, bad been divided into four schedules and in respect of one, amounting to .£l5 is Sd, the judgment creditor agreed tbat no claim could be substantiated.

Mr Mason went into the witness b )x. and before he had completed his evidence Mr Miller announced that a settlernlnt of the action had been agreed upon. The terms were that an order should be issued for payment to the execution creditor of £35, the balance to go to the claimant. The execu- tion creditor was to pay the Sheriff's costs, but there was no order as to the costs of the interpleader actions.

NEW PRIZE SCHEME

FOR ESSAYS ON INSURANCE ACT.

CASH PRIZES FOR OUR READERS.

In order to encourage the study and under- standing of the National Insurance Act we have decided to offer two special prizes of .£2 and £1 for essays on the Act, contributed by our readers.

At the present time a series of special articles on the Act, written by leading Members of Parliament and other authorities, is appearing weekly in the " Examiner," and in order to give intending competitors a chance to study the question of National Insurance in all its bearings, we have decided to keep the competition open till Saturday, May 11th. All essays must be original,and must reach this office, addressed to the Editor "Examiner," on or before Saturday, May 11th. The name and address of the con- tributor must be enclosed with the essay. The essays should not exceed 2,000 words in length, and should bear on the ;following points: (1) How the Act will affect any class of worker in your constituency. (2) How it will affect Trade Unionists.

(3) How it will affect workers in a special industry or the industry which they are engaged. In awarding the prizes, the first considera- tion will not be literary merit, but the use- fulness of the essay as a general practical explanation of the benefits of the Act, and especially as it may affect the particular trade or occupation with which the writer may deal. Competitors must write on one side of the paper only. The two prize essays will be published subsequently in "The Examiner. " The Editor's award is to be considered final.

YESTERDAY'S FOOTBALL.

Warrington v. Leeds.

This popular fixture was played at Wilderspool yesterdpy atternoon, in fine but windy weather, before is crowd of 5,000. Owing to the strike a reduction in prices of admission was made and the concession was d +ubtless much appreciated. Leeds bad difficulty in making the journey, but getting as far as Liverpool, they motored from the Mersey City. They brought a represtntative team, including Ganley, the ex-Leigh half-baclr. Warrington were disappointed by the failure of Jolley to turn out at halt-back, and Jordan again deputised. Warrington started against the wind and Ganley almost immediately broke through cleverly and passed to Gilliee, who was well tackled by Tilley. Warrington were forced to the line and Renwick ot off-side in attempting to clear, but Ganley hit the posts. War- rington eventually got clear through a penalty for Tilley, and play was removed to centre. He .ton soon returned with a powerful punt to the corner,and Gillies dropped a gal the next minute. Warrington responded finely. Mclntyre:and Cheaters forcing play to the line. Renwick tried to rush over but was held. A penalty lost the advantage and Leeds attacked again, but the Warrington men defended finely, Tilley making a clear- ance which was cheered to the echo. Ganley tried to kick out, but Dickeneon fielded finely and dashed through the Leeds backs to score a great try. ~"Renwick missed the goal, but Warringtou were a poiut up against the wind. Leeds attacked persistently, and eventually Ganley threw to Dennis, , 1 who crossed at the corner. Half-time : Leeds, five ' points ; Warrington, 3 points. Warrington,soon took the offensive, and Daintith broke through, Cheaters nearly crossing at the corner. Then Jordan tried to rush over, and Leeds were penalised, Renwick equalising within five minutes. Then Renwick broke through and dribbled over, but lost the touch under the posts—bard luck ! The next minute Renwick saved Warrington when Tilley was beaten. Again was Renwizik to the fore with a great run to the line. Barron relieved, and then Cheaters, Thomas and Mclntyre ran half the length of the field, the latter scoring behind the posts, Renwick goal- ing. Warrington were now overplaying Leeds in every way. Cheaters ought to have had another but pissed forward on the line. Leeds rushed back and Ganley cross kicked for Harrison to score under the posts—an unexpected try. Ganlidy equal;sei the scores again. Play now became very keen and Renwick again ran down and Ganley was penalised close in, Renwick kicking another goal amid great cheering. Leeds were not done with, and Ganley broke clean through, but Renwick woe the race by a yard and kicked dead. Play continued exciting and Dickenson intercepted at half-way and raced through for Chestera to score under the bar, Renwick again goaling. A minute later Daintith dropped a goal. Final, War- rington 5 goals, 3 tries, 19 points; Leeds, 2 goals, 2 tries, 10 points.

FISH'S TEAM V. RYLANDS' RECS.

Four thousand spect4tors displayed great interest in a match played at Gorey-larie yesterday morning between a team of old Warrington players captained by Fish, and Itylands Recreation. There was great cheering when Fish opened the scoring with a goal and a try, but Rylands' men were naturally in better train- ing and eventually ran out winners by 14 points to 5. The wire-men played smart football and deserved their success. Fish (who retired hurt in the second half) Cal twright and Clare were the best of the old brigade.

reatest Problem of the Day. The month that has just passed (says St. James's Pariah Magazine) will be memorable for very many years at the time of the great ct al strike. Anyone can see that the greatest problem that awaits solution in England at present is the settlement of industrial disputes and the prevention of these disastrous strikes which paralyze the trade of the country and inflict cruel suffering on innumerable people. Well may we pray " Give peace in our time 0 Lord."

The Church and Health Week. It is proposed (says the " Warrington Parish Church Magazine ") to observe April 28th to May 4th as a " Health Week," that is to say as a time for bringing before the notice of all inhabitants of Warrington the immense importance of healthy homes and pure Ayes. On Sunthy, Apiil 28th, at 3 p.m., there will be a gath ing of Church men's Bible classes at the Parish Chur and of Church women's Bible classes at St. Paul's. A preacher will be provided at both churchee,and we trust all the parishes will send contingents. The Labour Trouble.

In a reference to the labour war in the current issue of St. Paul's Parish Magazine the Rev W Bracecamp writes :—The only thing we can all do is to pray, and pray earnestly, Cult God in His great mercy and love will bring about peace. We are in danger of ignoring the first principles of our religion, viz., the true brotherhood if man." The success of our country from a commercial point of view, no doubt, is important ; but a far more important th'ng, to my mind, is that the individual vtho helps to make up a nation, should learn his indenendence upon God, end how important, too, it is that the principles cf God's holy religion should be more r ractised in ever-ay life. Before many hours have passed I trust we may hear Oat a settlea ent has been arrived at ; in the meantime let us pray from our hearts that' God may have mercy upc n us."

"The Class War." Rev. Dr. Mellor on Syndicalism. In the course of a sermon Os " L'he class war" at Cairo-street Chapel on Sunday evening the Rev. Dr. Stanley Mellor referred at souse length to syndicalism. it Was, be said, a three and a dangerous gospel and yet, heaven knew, it was not without justification. It was to the last degree revolutionary, utterly devoid of sentimental feeling for the community as a whole, utterly intent on the achievement of one single end. The only social force which it recognized seas class interest. It was obvious that a company of men possessed by that gospel might go a very long way ; we were having experience of how far men could go in whose minds that g ispel of open war was only dimly and vaguely present. The syndicalist gospel of open class war was very far from being complete in detail. It expressed at the moment rather a spirit that a settled policy, and had its sources in disappointment and a growing sense of injustica, rather than in any c.refully considered and systematic argument. Nor was he prepared to say that, even in such completeness as it had so far attained, it was altogether the gospel of the working classes. It might perhaps be little more than a name, but there could be no doubt that at least t sao of the fundamental ideas of syndicalism had penetrated to the depths of the world's masses, and could he traced at the back of all the unrest and disturbance in the midst of which we were living. On the one hand there was the idea that labour had an absolute right to the whole of its product, add owed no duty to itself or the world other than the assertion of orifeithoe tthheatdresigthrut.ctiCoanr organization. Fureherrcore the essentially syndicalist u tb atsoi i ot sf loougri c pa rl e is sesnute , ci nmoema ot notion was abroad in the world that industries ought to be managed and controlled by those who worked them—not, in general, by an outside agency or board of directors, but by the workers themselves. That notion pointed back to something that was really fine and magnificent— the new conscious- ness of the worker that "he knows his work and no one else knows it " and that, therefore, "be ought to have, in every sense, an interest in its organization." That meant that the worker was alive to a wholly new sense of his valae, that he war inspired by a new sense of personality—a fact fraught with an amazing signific- ance for life generally. What had the Church to say to these ideas? They seemed at first sight utterly to contradict her favourite gospel. They preached hatred and not love ! But it was hatred of uttruth and injustice; hatred of conditions that made life ugly, mean and wretched ; hatred of an order that destroyed freedom and crushed individuality. Was the Church to refuse her blessing to hatred of that kind ? Woe be to her, if she did ! It was hatred also which really rested on love—love of freedom, love of the individual, love of humanity. if the Church saw aright, it would he clear to her that the love which, for so long, she had taught was at last perhaps beginning to work. And if all that meant conflict, war and not peace, it was idle for the Church to preach peace when there could he no peace. All those who belonged In any way to the Church, all religious men and women everywhere, must recognize the truth of the world's ;Rtehveo shei et idosn aorfy v leeans c e hadsa d dente e s tr re ud tti ohne gospel. It was to late to ignore it, to late to prevent hhiteasdarbtgsersoonwf tsmho.ewnn. He did not think that anything would save atsh e doomedestabli s h and dcotl orderio s d neconomic organization was destined for a total change, not slllrivrie ave. s "' Our n knew n whole economic if not for a complete fall. Established interests, no they might be,could not battle success- mfualltyteargbaoinw dstvesasrt, creative ideas. If the confl ct ended merely in conflict and nothing else—whose would betbe blame?ltwould not rest upon the shoulders of those whose hca-ts had been moved by great ideas, who bad felt the pain of injustice and the love of freedom and bad not stopped short of violence in the assertion of themselves No, the blame would rest with those who were too narrow-minded, too small-souled, too unimaginative to feel and see the truth in time and wisely to direct affairs by submitting. The sin would be upon "the rich, the educated and the powerful," who, ashen they might have seen, refused, and when they might have words:—" Thshould erioutsafkae to cto had the vision, blinded their eyes in seltflstmess. He saw some words the other day which should be written in letters of fire at every street corner and in every in heart, theblic appnrdleasyceeentt: quiteswi uradtssi iomnwphilse wrd i cheveryone not the unrtst of labour. it is the blind and selfish eg itism of property." Who better than the Church could reprove and check that selfishness and egotism ? What voice could utter word of warning more, potent that that of religion ? Here was work at the moment for the Church and for religion. On the one hand men must be made fit hi themselves to raise the cry for freedom. They must be rendered able in themselves, worthy to make war upon their circumstances. The power of religion could make a man most worthy. On the other hand the blindness of selfishness, the lethargy of ease and comfort must be warned and checked in time, lest the worst befall.

LE ZOUTE-KNOCKE -NEAR OSTEND— The recent weather has been splendid for the golf course, which is in tine condition. It is said by those who should know, to be one of the best IS hole courses on the Continent, and has that additional charm for all lovers of the health-giving game of being close to the shore, where there is a beautiful beach of fine white sand some fifty yards wide at low tide, and extending about twenty-five miles along the coast, running by Ostend and Blankenberghe and along to the frontier of 1-{oll4nd, only a short distance from I.e Zoute-Knocke. Owing to the fine beach and good bathing, thiu golfing station is a great favourite with the children.
